The OFF switch exists in the harness adapter ONLY; the mod's class has none, deliberately — a switch readable from a server's environment would silently reinstate the defect. **Java override executed in-game 2026-09-19** on OC 1.12.61-GTNH with jar `c9c5cd8`: the REPL ran `for i = 1, 2000 do local h = io.open(...); h:read(1); h:close() end` — in `SynchronizedCall` for nearly its whole duration — and the world was saved mid-loop. On reload **the loop resumed and finished**, the marker survived, the earlier handle round-trip still worked, and the log carries no state-load error. The state directory is the signature: `_kernel` 183139 B written at the save, and **no `_stack`** — the only `_stack` present is byte-identical (md5 `cff7b574`) to the 09-16 file, its mtime bumped by `SaveHandler.onWorldLoad`. Under the old code that save would have written a ~183 KB `_stack`.
- [ ] **`W6b` is a timing assertion that fails on a contended host** (4M iterations in 61.7 ms against a threshold wanting milliseconds; trace counts unchanged at 1/1/0, so the JIT is fine and only the wall clock moved). Third one of these after `d-autorun-counter-live` and `p0` — worth a general answer rather than a third special case: either scale such bounds by a measured host factor, or skip them when a load flag is set. **Fourth, 2026-09-19:** `k4-still-compiled-after-timeout` failed once under a mild JVM load at 0.0106 s against its 0.010 s bound (quiet 0.005 s), while the same run's stats read `hooked=false` — the bound's own explanation ("the count=1 re-arm survived disarm") was contradicted by the run's own evidence. Same answer applies. Also from that matrix: the harness's boot and autorun caps are counted in ticks, so under load they close before OpenOS gets there (3 of 4 runs under 16 JVM threads) — the caps should be uptime- or wall-based when a load flag is set. **Fifth, 2026-09-29, on a FASTER host rather than a busier one — and k3/k4 got the general answer.** The first harness runs on the new machine (Core Ultra 9 285HX) read `k4 NEGATIVE-CONTROL: FAIL -- jit=off: loop after the timeout = 0.0097 (must NOT be compiled-fast)`: the JIT-off interpreter had moved under the 0.010 s bound that the old box's 0.017–0.026 s had set, and the k3 negative control was at 0.0113/0.0125 against the same bound. The loop itself was the weak instrument: `s + (i % 7) * 2` is fmod-bound in both modes (compiled and interpreted only ~3x apart) and a compiled 2M loop is ~3 ms, which read 0.0025–0.0071 s in-machine across runs. **Now** ([research/hook-vs-jit.md](research/hook-vs-jit.md), 2026-09-29 addendum): the probe is four independent accumulator chains over 12M iterations (compiled ~10x faster than interpreted), min of up to three reps stopping after 0.6 s, with the exact sum carried and checked; the harness times THE SAME loop interpreted in the raw state of the same run (`jit.off(work)`) and every k3/k4 bound is a ratio to it — compiled-fast under a third, not compiled at or above. Unchanged and still absolute: W6b's `dt < 20.0` ms (`test/native/wd_test.c:319`; also one of the three detectors in `negative-control.sh:721`'s standing-hook control, which its trace-count half alone would still catch), the boot cap of 600/3000 ticks (`OcljSmoke.scala:4224`), `d-autorun-counter-live`'s 300/2000-tick wait (`:4282`) and p0's 800-tick wait (`:4655`). - [x] **OC's DEADLINE HOOK AND THE JIT ARE IN DIRECT CONFLICT, and this is bigger than anything else on this list** ([research/hook-vs-jit.md](research/hook-vs-jit.md)). `machine.lua:1531` arms `debug.sethook(co, checkDeadline, "", hookInterval)` before every sandbox resume and **never clears it** — unlike `:847`/`:850`, which do pair arm with clear. An armed count hook makes `lj_dispatch.c:121` force instruction dispatch for the whole VM, and the CHECKHOOK patch we require in order to boot makes compiled traces exit to the interpreter on entry. CHECKHOOK's own comment says the quiet part: *"this is only useful if hooks are **not** set most of the time"* (`lj_record.c:2953-2965`) — it was written for an asynchronous Ctrl-C, and OC holds it armed continuously. Measured on our own `luajit.exe`, a 4M-iteration loop in a coroutine: JIT with no hook 0.008 s / 2 traces; interpreter 0.030 s; **JIT with the hook armed 0.928 s and 204 traces** — 31x SLOWER THAN TURNING THE JIT OFF, because traces compile, get bounced by the guard on entry, never abort (so never blacklist) and are recorded again forever. The JIT is not disabled by the hook; it is thrashing. **This is the feasibility study's own "real crux" resurfacing** — it named exactly this and the correct design (no standing hook; an async watchdog injects `lua_sethook(count=1)` on expiry; CHECKHOOK build), and `prototype/watchdog/` validated it on hardware. The binding took the expedient route (OC's stock `machine.lua`, standing hook and all) to get booting. **The plan is in hook-vs-jit.md §5**: **step 0 DONE — CONFIRMED in a real machine** (hook-vs-jit.md §6; the harness carries `OCLJ_JIT=on|off` and a `JIT PROBE:` line): inside the sandbox, under OC's real hook, the same loop is **18.8x slower with the JIT on** than with it off (0.485 s vs 0.026 s), **OpenOS boots 40% slower** (6.6 s vs 4.7 s), and ~2 700 traces are recorded and discarded per boot; stable to ~1% over two runs each; **steps 1-2 DONE (hook-vs-jit.md §7)**: the watchdog lives in the shim as `_OCLJ_WATCHDOG` (Win32 timer queue; the callback's only act is `lua_sethook(count=1)`; the hook calls OC's own unchanged `checkDeadline`), and `native/kernel/patch-machine-lua.lua` derives the kernel from OC's `machine.lua` at four anchored sites. Measured in a real machine, three runs: the sandbox loop that was 0.473 s under OC's kernel is **0.003-0.004 s** — 170x faster, and 6x faster than the interpreter; OpenOS boots in 4.7 s instead of 6.55 s; ~110 traces completed per boot instead of ~2 500 discarded; `too long without yielding` still fires (k1). `wd_test.c` 32/32, smoke 26/26 (6/6 consecutive runs), 14 negative controls, and `race_test.c` — which reproduces the cross-thread `HOOK_ACTIVE` wedge on the OLD injection (4/4 in 34-792 ms) and shows the current atomic-OR one surviving 119 393 re-fires. Adversarial review done: 5 lenses, 65 verify verdicts, 7 confirmed findings, all fixed or recorded (hook-vs-jit.md §7). **Step 3 Phase 0 DONE** ([bench/results-in-machine-2026-09-03.md](../bench/results-in-machine-2026-09-03.md)): inside a real machine, against ocelot-brain's bundled PUC Lua 5.2 (what players run today), compute-bound work is **17.5x** faster with the JIT on (2.58x of that is the interpreter plus the hook removal, 6.79x the compiler) and component-bound work is **1.00x** -- flat, because an indirect component call costs a game tick whatever the VM. The predicted bimodal shape, measured. The checksum control was observed to reject a sabotaged benchmark that ran 4.7x "faster". **Step 3 Phase 1 BUILT, not yet run in a machine**: `bench/oc/` holds nine benchmarks plus a pinned `compat.lua`, `references.txt` (the suite definition the harness builds its manifest from) and `run-standalone.sh`, which fails if any CHECK disagrees between VMs or with the reference file — it passes, on four VMs. Three rows keep their PUBLISHED references (`mandelbrot`, `nqueens`, `sha256`). Standalone the spread is 120× wide and bimodal exactly as predicted: sha256 33.5×, mandelbrot 13.7×, trampoline 12.9×, matmul 11.4×, sieve 9.2×, binarytrees 3.8×, strings2 3.4×, nqueens 1.1×, and **`strings` 0.27× — the compiler LOSING**, preserved through the rescale (published 0.25×). The pathology pair is the suite's only oracle not resting on a single implementation: two codings, one checksum, 13× apart. `strings` is QUARANTINED (`!` prefix) because it allocates 3064 KB at return against a machine with 865–1024 KB free; `OCLJ_BENCH_ONLY=strings` runs it alone, since it is expected to kill the machine and the persist milestones run after the suite. The harness gained the suite driver (one benchmark per timer callback → a fresh 5 s deadline each, so an overrun costs one row not the run), a RAM guard, and the **encore** probe that finally measures post-save recovery — it rides on eris by being a repeating timer holding a closure, so it survives the save and fires again unprompted. **Step 3 Phase 1 RAN 2026-09-04** ([bench/results-in-machine-phase1-2026-09-04.md](../bench/results-in-machine-phase1-2026-09-04.md)): 78 runs, one benchmark per freshly booted 1 MB machine, three cells interleaved, three replicates, each boot calibrated by a fixed 2M-iteration box-speed meter. **B/C -- the clean compiler experiment, same binary and kernel and source with only jit.off() differing -- is 22.6x on sha256 and 5.13x on mandelbrot**, reproducing the standalone ratios (23.1x, 5.49x); against the PUC 5.2 players run today, mandelbrot is 12.3x and matmul 11.0x. Two failures matter more than the table: **`sieve` completes on PUC 3 of 3 and dies of `not enough memory` on our native 6 of 6** (the §8 emergency-GC divergence, now a benchmark rather than an argument), and **`strings` is unrunnable with the compiler ON and fine with it OFF** while its identical-checksum twin runs everywhere. And the ceiling: **`trampoline` is 12x slower in-machine than standalone-interpreted**, so on pcall-heavy work the sandbox, not the compiler, decides. No geomean. **RE-RUN 2026-09-04 on a fixed harness, and three things changed.** The first table carried n=1 and n=2 rows throughout — a harness bug was reading the machine's Lua state while ocelot-brain's executor used it, discarding ~1 run in 4; every row is now n=3 across 78 runs with no wedge. **`nqueens` is withdrawn as not quotable**: cell C varies 2.05x *within a single boot*, so the published 1.44x and the re-run's 0.70x are both inside its own noise, and the "sign reversal" was never a sign. **The `sieve` measurement is stronger, not weaker**: the six matrix cells that came back `SKIP-LOWMEM` were a 3x UNITS BUG in the RAM guard (the peak column is real KB; `computer.freeMemory()` is real free divided by `ramScaleFor64Bit` — a 1024 KB machine holds 3072 real KB), and with that fixed the guard admits the workload and the machines answer: PUC finishes in 2.65-2.84 s with 676-805 KB free, ours dies 6 of 6, cell B with `jit.status()=false` so no trace memory is involved. Its live set is 52.4 KB against a 3042 KB budget, so the heap reached ~58x its live set — over 98% garbage at the refusal. The guard's peak column cannot be fixed (every sampler is a GC safepoint, so the figure tracks the sample rate and the unsampled heap *at return* exceeds the sampled *peak*), so `sieve` is now quarantined by hand like `strings`. **Next**: the emergency-GC mode (now the best-evidenced item on this list), then the census OSes. **Done -- verified 2026-10-03:** No standing hook remains. The derived kernel replaces OC's `debug.sethook(co, checkDeadline, "", hookInterval)` (ocelot-brain `machine.lua:1531`) with `watchdog.arm`/`disarm` around every sandbox resume (`build/native/kernel/machine.lua:1625-1627`, also `:751`, `:883`), refuses to run without `_OCLJ_WATCHDOG` (`:57-59`), and arms through `native/lj52shim.c:1850-1942`. Gated by harness `k1-deadline-still-fires` (`OcljSmoke.scala:4548`) and by `k2-jit-not-thrashing`, the ratio-judged `k3`/`k4`, and `k5` (`:4386`, `:4388`, `:4637`, `:4597`), each of these four with a negative control, plus `wd_test.c`, `race_test.c` and the standing-hook sabotage in `negative-control.sh:472`. It was measured in the 09-03/09-04 in-machine results and in-game T4/T5/T7. The trailing 'Next' entries are tracked in the emergency-GC row and in the MineOS-measurements and 5.2-class-VM rows (census OSes).
- [ ] **WE ARE A 5.2-CLASS VM AND MUCH OF THE ECOSYSTEM IS 5.3 -- measured 2026-09-15** ([research/os-shape-census.md](research/os-shape-census.md), "Booting them"). QuickOS dies at boot on our native with `attempt to index global 'utf8'` and reaches a shell on PUC 5.3 -- the baseline arm that turns this from a code-reading into a measurement. It is NOT our defect: `machine.lua:1033` exposes `utf8 = utf8 and {...}`, i.e. only if the host VM has it, and `LuaStateFactory` opens `Library.UTF8` for 5.3/5.4 but `Library.BIT32` for 5.2, so QuickOS fails identically on OpenComputers' OWN 5.2 native. But it is a **product-shaping** fact: LuaJIT is 5.1 plus partial 5.2 compat -- no `utf8`, no integer subtype, no `//` -- so a LuaJIT architecture is 5.2-class by construction, and 5.3 has been OC's default for a long time. **MITIGATION, cheap and bounded**: because the sandbox writes `utf8 = utf8 and {...}`, the table merely has to EXIST in the raw state. A Lua `utf8` (`char`, `codes`, `codepoint`, `len`, `offset`, `charpattern`) installed at `luaopen` time, the way the shim's `OCLJ_JITOFF` is so it survives `eris`, removes one known barrier. **It would not make QuickOS work** -- `utf8` is the FIRST 5.3 dependency it hits, not necessarily the only one, and the depth of the rest is unmeasured at one run per fix. Decide deliberately how far to chase 5.3 compatibility; it bounds the addressable content. **Status 2026-10-03:** not started, and two things in the row need settling first. The precedent it cites does not exist: the shim has no `OCLJ_JITOFF` (`lj52shim.c:12-19` lists it among the removed `getenv` hatches, and `build-native.sh:313` refuses the token); the real precedent for a raw-state global that survives eris is `_OCLJ_NATIVE`/`_OCLJ_WATCHDOG`, set in `lj52_newstate` (`lj52shim.c:2386-2392`). The same false precedent is repeated at [research/os-shape-census.md](research/os-shape-census.md):203 and in a comment at `OcljSmoke.scala:5276`. And the code argues against the mitigation: `LuaJITStateFactory.java:91-95` opens BIT32 and not UTF8 on purpose, because adding UTF8 "to look more capable would make us a fourth dialect that no OS targets". Settle that disagreement before building anything. Also tracked here, moved from the `_ENV` row: re-check `utf8`, `string.pack` and `math.maxinteger` the way `_ENV` should have been checked, by grepping OpenOS rather than the kernel ([research/report-compat-and-perf.md](research/report-compat-and-perf.md):93 still calls them optional or guarded). **Status 2026-10-10:** GTNH's default CPU is Lua 5.3, which has `utf8` (OC application.conf:232 `defaultLua53`, also set in the user's GTNH config; LuaStateFactory.scala:89 opens UTF8 for it; verified 2026-10-10). The pinned LuaJIT 1ee778a4 contains upstream a2ce8114: it parses 5.3's `& | ~ << >>` but not `//`, and has no `utf8` (verified 2026-10-10 with the build's luajit.exe); it also parses `continue`, `+=`, `?.`, `??`, `!=`, `&&` and `||` (same check), which no other OC CPU does, so "5.2-class" holds for the library set, not for the syntax. The false `OCLJ_JITOFF` precedent was also at [research/memory-accounting.md](research/memory-accounting.md):643, corrected there 2026-10-10. The OpenOS re-check is done, and report-compat-and-perf.md:93 holds for all three (grep of ocelot-brain's and GTNH's OpenOS, 2026-10-10): `utf8` appears only as `utf8 and ...` (lib/keyboard.lua:49), `math.maxinteger` only as `math.maxinteger or math.huge` (init.lua:7, lib/core/install_basics.lua:119), `string.pack` nowhere; the one `//` (lib/bit32.lua:26) loads only when there is no global `bit32` (lib/package.lua:13), which ours has (LuaJITStateFactory.java:100; build/native/kernel/machine.lua:1010). By grep of the census tree, QuickOS's only unguarded 5.3 library use is `utf8.len` (lib/core/boot.lua:42); whether the mitigation boots it is unmeasured (one census run). **Status 2026-10-16:** the 2026-10-13 decision settles the disagreement above: our LuaJIT is a fork that may take 5.3 functions, so the "fourth dialect" stance at `LuaJITStateFactory.java:91-95` no longer binds; that comment changes with the first 5.3 addition (`string.pack` is next). The native is now built without FFI (the FFI row under v1), so the 5.3 operators lost their 64-bit operand form with the literals: on HEAD 62970a0 `1LL << 40` was the cdata `1099511627776LL`; now `1LL` is a syntax error, and `& | ~ << >>` compute 32-bit results only (`1 << 32` gives 1) until the decided fix to the bitwise operators. **Status 2026-10-17:** `string.pack`, `string.unpack` and `string.packsize` are in, the first 5.3 functions our fork adds (the row directly after the FFI row under v1 has the detail). They are library functions of LuaJIT's own string library, registered in the build copy's `lib_string.c` (`native/luajit/patch-strpack-register.sh`; the code is `native/luajit/oclj_strpack.c`), so luajit.exe, both natives and every native test get them from one `luaopen_string`, and `('<i4'):pack(1)` works through the string metatable, as in 5.3. The kernel needed no change: it already copied all three into the sandbox (`build/native/kernel/machine.lua:946-948`). `LuaJITStateFactory.java`'s comment no longer claims OC's 5.2 library set "exactly": it is OC's 5.2 list of libraries, with 5.3 functions inside them. Still absent: `utf8`, `math.type`, `math.tointeger`, `math.maxinteger`, the integer subtype and `//`; the sandbox's `_VERSION` stays "Lua 5.2" (machine.lua:857). Plan9k takes `string.pack` as its 5.3 test: `lib/modules/base/10_procfs.lua:110` reports microcode 0x53, and `lib/msdosfs.lua:23` takes its pack path, which inherits a FAT12 `mkdir` bug of Plan9k's own (the string.pack row under v1). **Found on the way, not fixed:** on Windows, LuaJIT's `tonumber(s, base)` saturates at 4294967295 (`lib_base.c:301-307` uses `strtoul`, and `unsigned long` is 32 bits on Win64): `tonumber("0123456789abcdef", 16)` gives 4294967295 where PUC 5.3.6 gives 81985529216486895 (measured 2026-10-17), so the same program gives different results on Windows and Linux servers, and Plan9k's `uuidBin` (`lib/modules/base/01_util.lua:66-71`, no caller) would pack a wrong UUID whatever `pack` does. (Upstream's `2a8e017`, 2026-10-14, after our pin, uses `strtoull` there; by reading it removes the saturation, unbuilt here: row 214 (4).) The `LuaJITStateFactory.java:100` above (BIT32) is now `:114`. **Status 2026-10-17, round 3:** the 2026-10-16 sentence above is resolved. `& | ~ << >>` and unary `~` now compute Lua 5.3.6's 64-bit integer results on plain numbers, which no earlier build did (before FFI was removed, only cdata operands were 64-bit, by FFI's rules), in the parser's folding, the interpreter and the JIT (the bitwise-operators row under v1, after the string.pack row): `1 << 32` is 4294967296. A result with more than 53 significant bits, which no double holds, raises `result of '<op>' has more than 53 significant bits (mask each operand to 32 bits first)` (decided 2026-10-17, the hint the same day), so a 5.3 program that shifts a negative number right (`-1 >> 1`, 5.3's `math.maxinteger` idiom `~0 >> 1`) or rotates a 32-bit word the usual way (`((x >> n) | (x << (32 - n))) & 0xFFFFFFFF` with n from 1 to 10) raises here until it masks first. Of the syntax extensions above, the bitwise compound assignments (`x <<= 40`) and LuaJIT's `~>>` compute 64-bit results by the same rules. Still absent: `utf8`, `math.type`, `math.tointeger`, `math.maxinteger`, the integer subtype, `//` and the bitwise metamethods (the last two planned together, the metamethods row under v1). `LuaJITStateFactory.java`'s comment now names the operators, and BIT32 is at `:119`.
- [ ] **MINEOS DOES NOT BOOT ON THE ARCHITECTURE WE SHIP -- found 2026-10-03.** `Libraries/Color.lua:11` tests `computer.getArchitecture() ~= "Lua 5.2"` and, on anything else, `load`s its Lua 5.3 bitwise code; on "LuaJIT" that source cannot compile, `load` returns nil, and the call at `:63` dies (`attempt to call a nil value`) inside `/OS.lua`'s `UIRequire`, so the desktop never comes up (2 x 1024 KB at 1.8; stock PUC 5.2 ran the same tree with no error on screen: [bench/results-ramscale-2026-10-03.md](../bench/results-ramscale-2026-10-03.md), "The census OSes"). The 2026-09-15 census ran MineOS on the dropin arm, whose class reports "Lua 5.2", so the shipped architecture had never booted it. It is the only architecture-name test in MineOS (`Applications/Lua.app/Main.lua:14` only displays the name) and in the other census trees (AxisOS, QuickOS, OpenOS: none). Options, none chosen: an architecture name that such a test reads as 5.2 (but it is the player-visible identity in the CPU cycle and in `computer.getArchitecture()`); or an upstream fix in MineOS to test a capability (`load("return 1 >> 1")`) rather than a name. Also from that run: `CensusOs` scored it `no panic text on screen` -- its verdict does not look for a stack traceback, so a GUI OS that crashed into its own error handler passes. **Status 2026-10-10:** the diagnosis is wrong in one step: the bitwise operators compile on the pinned LuaJIT (1ee778a4 contains upstream a2ce8114); the chunk dies on floor division, `// 1.0` (`Libraries/Color.lua:28-30`, `:37-39`), which a2ce8114 did not backport (LuaJIT doc/extensions.html:432-434). Loaded with the build's luajit.exe, the chunk fails at its line 16 (`Color.lua:28`) with `unexpected symbol near '/'`, and compiles with `//` replaced by `/` (2026-10-10). So the probe above, `load("return 1 >> 1")`, compiles on ours and would still pick the broken chunk; an upstream fix has to probe `//`, or fall back when `load` returns nil. A rename to exactly "Lua 5.2" collides with OC's own architecture of that name (NativeLuaArchitecture.scala:23): `computer.setArchitecture` takes the first name match (ComputerAPI.scala:142), and MineOS's Settings module, a second name test that changes no code path, selects and switches CPUs by name (`Applications/Settings.app/Modules/8_System/Main.lua:19-28`), so one of the two could not be chosen there. Whether `//` is MineOS's only blocker on ours is unmeasured: one census with Color.lua's 5.2 path forced would tell. The `CensusOs` verdict gap (CensusOs.scala:417 looks only for `PANIC` and `not enough memory`): Merged 2026-10-10 into the false-green guard row. **Status 2026-10-17, round 3:** `//` is still the blocker. The bitwise operators now compute Lua 5.3.6's 64-bit results (the bitwise-operators row under v1), but loaded with round 3's prototype luajit.exe, the census tree's 5.3 chunk (the `load` at `Libraries/Color.lua:12-63`) still fails at its line 16 (`:28`) with `unexpected symbol near '/'`, as on the round-2 build, while PUC 5.3.6 loads it (2026-10-17). Its bitwise work is on 24-bit colours, non-negative and below 2^32, where every operator's result is exact, so by reading nothing in it would raise the new 53-bit error once `//` parses; not run. `//` comes with the bitwise metamethods, after the CRC32 follow-up (the metamethods row under v1).
- [x] **The RAM cap is structurally blind to JIT machine code.** mcode is `VirtualAlloc`'d and never passes `g->allocf` (`lj_mcode.c`; `maxmcode` 2048 KB at `lj_jit.h`), as is the FFI callback page. So "the cap is enforced" means "enforced for allocf traffic", and for a 1 MB machine the invisible ceiling exceeds the entire advertised RAM. Currently masked by the deadline-hook row — traces barely run — which is an accident, not a mitigation. Measure `J->szallmcarea` after a boot rather than assuming. **Status 2026-10-03:** Measured, not bounded. `_OCLJ_JITSTATS` reads `J->szallmcarea` (`native/lj52shim.c:1966-2009`), harness `m1-mcode-is-real` (`OcljSmoke.scala:4960`, JIT-off negative control `:4955`) gates it, and a booted OpenOS holds 196 608 B uncharged (hook-vs-jit.md §8). The 'masked' premise is stale: traces have run since the watchdog kernel. Nothing sets `JIT_P_maxmcode` (no `jit.opt` call in `native/`, the kernel or `src/`), so each machine can still reach the 2048 KB default (`lj_jit.h:128`) outside the cap; the 09-22 pressure flush frees mcode only as a side effect of a trigger that sees `allocf` headroom alone, and the remedy is the jit.opt tuning row. The FFI callback page is moot, because `ffi` is never opened (`LuaJITStateFactory.java:98-107`). **Status 2026-10-10:** Merged 2026-10-10 into the jit.opt tuning row, which carries its mcode facts with current figures (128 KB after boot, 704 KB after a save) and the bound still to set. **Correction 2026-10-16:** the callback page was unreachable because a callback needs `ffi.cast`, not because `ffi` was never opened: the lexer opened it on demand for a `1LL` in sandboxed code. Since 2026-10-16 the native has no FFI at all, so no callback page exists (`lj_ccallback.c` is compiled out). **Status 2026-10-17:** the `LuaJITStateFactory.java:98-107` above is now `:112-121` (the factory comment grew with the string.pack row).
- [x] **Memory accounting — DONE, and it landed with the pushcfunction window as one change** ([research/memory-accounting.md](research/memory-accounting.md)). OC's per-machine RAM cap is now enforced, not merely reported. `kernelMemory` went from its literal floor of **1** to **323 809**, `freeMemory` falls as OpenOS boots, and an unbounded allocation stops **at** the cap and surfaces as `LuaMemoryAllocationException` -> `ExecutionResult.Error("not enough memory")`, exactly as on stock OpenComputers. Evidence: `test/native/mem_test.c` (11 checks, hermetic, no JVM), 18/18 in the ocelot-brain smoke test (was 13), and four sabotages in `test/native/negative-control.sh` — one of which must **kill the test process**, which is the JVM-killer demonstrated rather than described.
  - The mechanism, in one line: one allocator installed at `lj52_newstate` and never replaced, so `lua_getallocf` recovers its per-state record from any thread with no table and no locking; jnlua's `lua_setallocf` calls only flip a flag; and the allocator uses **jnlua's own** `getluamemory`/`setluamemory`/`getthreadenv`, handed to it by a macro that expands inside `jnlua.c` where those statics are in scope. It never touches the Lua API — which is the actual fix, because `getjavastate`'s `lua_getfield` from inside a `lua_Alloc` callback is what took the JVM down (`java exit=127`, no `hs_err`, no exception). **2026-10-03:** jnlua's accessors are now the legacy path only. Since 6edc5e0 the additive class hands the cap to the shim, and from then on `lj52_alloc` touches only C fields (`lj52shim.c:392-435`; [accounting-sync.md](accounting-sync.md)); the accessors serve the dropin and any state that has not handed over.
  - The EAGER warm-up the previous plan called for **cannot be written**: the 38 targets are file-static in `jnlua.c`, and the macro that could name them expands at the push sites, not at newstate. Instead `lj52_pushcfunction` runs its whole body with refusal suspended — charged, never refused — bounded by a compile-time constant (38 distinct statics x 48 bytes; sandbox Lua cannot reach the function and cannot add a 39th).
  - Correction worth keeping: `lua_checkstack` is **not** a second bare-frame hazard on LuaJIT. It grows the stack through the *protected* `lj_state_cpgrowstack` and returns 0. A scan of every `JNIEXPORT` entry point confirms `lua_pushcfunction` is the only bare-frame `LUA_ERRMEM` source in `jnlua.c`.
  - Also measured: on Win x64 with `LJ_UNWIND_EXT`, `lua_atpanic` **never fires** on this path — the throw becomes a `RaiseException` nothing catches and the OS terminates the process. That is why the death had no diagnostic at all, and why the shim's panic handler could not have produced one.
- [x] **Decide the RAM scale our architecture defaults to — OC's stock 1.8 is NOT enough.** `ramScaleFor64Bit` is how many real bytes OC charges per apparent byte of installed RAM; OC ships 1.8, calibrated for 64-bit PUC Lua. LuaJIT GC64 needs more: the OC kernel alone measures ~320 KB. Measured on a 1024 KB machine booting OpenOS 1.8.9 — **1.8 boots 1 time in 6**, 2.5 boots 6/6, 3.0 boots 5/5. `test/native/smoke-test.sh` therefore pins the scale (`OCLJ_RAM_SCALE`, default 3.0) instead of inheriting it; `OCLJ_RAM_SCALE=1.8` reproduces the failure. Two things make this need a deliberate decision rather than a guess: the next item (there is no second chance at the wall), and the fact that the baseline OC sizes every machine from is not deterministic — `kernelMemory` ranged 296 493 to 395 089 across runs of the same build. **Plan in memory-accounting.md §11**: measure peak `used` over a full boot of OpenOS and the three census OSes, set our default from that with margin; ~1.8 × 1.85 (our kernel vs PUC's) ≈ 3.3 is the knob doing its job, not a fudge. One day, not a blocker. **Status 2026-10-03:** not started, and the boot counts above (1.8 boots 1 time in 6, 2.5 6/6, 3.0 5/5) are dated. They were measured on 2026-09-03 (410b3ee), before the emergency collector (2026-09-15), the per-machine arena (2026-10-02) and the shim-held accounting (2026-10-03), and nothing has re-run it; the first in-game run booted at 1.8 on a 16 MB machine, which says nothing about 1 MB. "No second chance at the wall" still holds in the narrowed form the next row records: the collector keeps the wall from being reached, and a refusal is not retried (`lj52shim.c:378-382`, and `:440-448` on the legacy path). **The plan also misses an implementation step.** OC sets `ramScale = Settings.ramScaleFor64Bit` in `initialize()` for every 64-bit native architecture (`NativeLuaArchitecture.scala:317` in the 1.12.55-GTNH sources), and `LuaJITArchitecture` never assigns it; it reads `ramScale()` only to convert the NBT `kernelMemory` (`:529`, `:614`). Changing OC's `ramScaleFor64Bit` would rescale OC's PUC CPUs too, so our default has to be set in our class after `super.initialize()`, or read from a key of our own, with that NBT conversion kept consistent. The harness's 3.0 was a pin in `smoke-test.sh` (OC's 1.8 since 2026-10-03), not a product default. **Done -- measured 2026-10-03: OC's own scale is enough, and the architecture inherits it** ([bench/results-ramscale-2026-10-03.md](../bench/results-ramscale-2026-10-03.md)). Both premises above were wrong. OC grants `kernelMemory` on top of the scaled RAM, so the kernel ratio is not what the scale pays for; and LuaJIT GC64 objects are mostly smaller than PUC 5.2's (`bench/oc/checks/objsize.lua`: OpenOS's code 0.69x, arrays and hash tables 0.51-0.87x, closures and strings 1.06-1.20x). Measured by where a program breaks -- `OCLJ_PROBE=capacity` grows a held structure until the allocator refuses -- at OC's 1.8 on 192, 256 and 1024 KB sticks ours holds at least what stock PUC 5.2 holds in every cell but closures at 1024 KB (about 0.92-0.98x once stock's own holder-doubling refusal is allowed for), counting only arms whose `kernelMemory` holds no kernel-init traces, with the JIT off as well as on, and the shipped configuration booted OpenOS in every run: the 1-in-6 above did not reproduce. So `LuaJITArchitecture` keeps OC's scale (no code, nothing new in NBT), and `smoke-test.sh` now defaults to 1.8, validated on all four arms (ours JIT on 65/0 twice, JIT off 63/0, sieve 59/0, stock 47/0; `mem-2`'s near-empty clause replaced a read of exactly 0 that raced the next recompile). The implementation step above is moot. What the probe found instead is the collector-at-the-wall row below, and MineOS's architecture-name test (the MineOS row).
- [x] **LuaJIT has no emergency GC, and under a hard cap that was a real divergence from PUC -- NARROWED 2026-09-15, built and measured** ([research/memory-accounting.md](research/memory-accounting.md) section 8f). `lj52_gc_pressure` in `native/lj52shim.c`: ~35 lines of shim, ZERO VM lines. It does NOT collect at the refusal -- section 11's C1, C5 and C6 each independently forbid that -- it writes two scalars the VM already owns (`gc.threshold = gc.total`, the trip-wire ~49 `lj_gc_check` sites test; and `gc.stepmul = 0`, which `lj_gc.c:734-736` turns into an unbounded step) and lets the VM collect at a safepoint. Disarms only on PROOF a cycle ran: the `gc.currentwhite` flip that only `atomic()` performs, plus a return to `GCSpause` -- because `gc_onestep` reaches `GCSpause` from `GCSsweep` and `GCSfinalize` WITHOUT calling `atomic()`, so the state alone would credit a sweep tail that re-marked nothing. Two traps caught in design review that a pass/fail benchmark would never have shown: writing `threshold = 0` instead of `gc.total` charges `debt += the whole heap` at `lj_gc.c:737-738` and pins the collector at 1024 bytes repaid per step for thousands of steps on the JIT-on path; and `GCSpause` is not evidence. RESULT, at DEFAULT pacing on the workload that died 6/6: **cell B survives 6/6 (1.47-1.52 s), cell C survives 6/6 (0.40-0.49 s against PUC's 2.65-2.84 s)**, with `arms` 134-160, `collects == arms` every run, **`bailouts == 0` and `refusals == 0`** -- the allocator never refused at all, so the wall is never reached rather than recovered from. `ENCORE_OK` throughout with nothing re-applied after the restore, closing section 8d's trap by construction. **NARROWED, NOT CLOSED**: the condition becomes `live + largest inter-safepoint burst <= cap` where PUC's is `live + largest single allocation <= cap`; the gap is irreducible without a finer safepoint, which would reintroduce C5 and C6. Accepted as the standing divergence earlier on 2026-10-03 (1314de5); the capacity probe that evening showed it is not rare (the collector-at-the-wall row). Its one observation is `sieveN8` at `pause=110`, a single run on 2026-09-15 (`bench/runs/2026-09-15-205900-gcfix-scale/rows.tsv:8`; [research/memory-accounting.md](research/memory-accounting.md):838-841: ours refused, PUC ran out of time instead), not re-run since the arena. Superseded text follows for the record:  `lmem.c`'s `luaM_realloc_` runs `luaC_fullgc(L, 1)` and RETRIES when an allocation is refused; `lj_mem_realloc` throws immediately. So a LuaJIT machine can be refused while holding a heap of garbage a collection would have reclaimed. Tuning the collector instead (`lua_gc(LUA_GCSETPAUSE, ...)` when a cap is armed) is safe and looked promising, but does **not** rescue the stock RAM scale: paced at pause 150 / stepmul 400 it booted 0 times in 3 at ramScale 1.8. It also buys whatever margin it does give with collector CPU, which is the opposite of this project's point. Measured and **not shipped**; calibration is the answer, not pacing. The faithful fix is an emergency mode in `lj_gc.c` that skips finalizers the way PUC's `isemergency` does; that is VM surgery, not a small patch — a milestone of its own with its own review, and nothing is gated on it (memory-accounting.md §11). **MEASURED IN A MACHINE 2026-09-04 and it is no longer a mechanism argument** (memory-accounting.md §8c): `sieve` at its shipped parameters completes on PUC 5.2 **3 of 3** (2.65-2.84 s, 676-805 KB free) and dies of `not enough memory` on our native **6 of 6**, in freshly booted 1 MB machines with the RAM guard admitting the workload. Cell B ran with `jit.status()=false` after a `jit.flush()`, so **no trace memory is involved** — plain table churn. The live set is **52.4 KB** against a **3042 KB** budget, so the heap reached ~58x its live set and was over 98% garbage when the allocator refused; `LUAI_GCPAUSE` does not bound this because `lj_gc.c:752-753` pins `threshold = gc.total` once the collector falls behind. And the sandbox cannot work around it: `machine.lua` builds the sandbox global table at `:737` and **`collectgarbage` is not in it**, while the kernel's own collect at `:1526` fires only between resumes and `sieve` finishes inside one. Two scoping notes that survive: `strings` with the JIT on is **out of reach of any collector** (pinned traces, §8a) and needs a trace tier instead; and §8d records a third, previously unwritten memory failure on the **persistence path**, JIT off, after a successful run — worth measuring before this is designed, since a collector that fires at the wall depends on how much of a restored heap is genuinely live. **Status 2026-10-10:** the `sieveN8` re-run, and with it this row's standing divergence, is the AxisOS and sieveN8 row.
- [ ] **AxisOS at 1 x 1024 KB and sieveN8 at pause=110, not re-run since the collector at the wall -- found 2026-10-03, added 2026-10-10.** (1) At OC's 1.8 on 2026-10-03, AxisOS on ours panicked "not enough memory" inside PatchGuard's Tier3 file hashing (20.5 s) after 1 refusal, with about a quarter of its cap free at the last sample, read as the collector-at-the-wall problem; stock could not parse PatchGuard, booted without it and panicked later, loading the GDI ([bench/results-ramscale-2026-10-03.md](../bench/results-ramscale-2026-10-03.md):179, :182-185). (2) `sieveN8` at `pause=110` is the emergency-GC row's one recorded refusal where PUC died of the deadline instead (`bench/runs/2026-09-15-205900-gcfix-scale/rows.tsv:8`, `bench/runs/2026-09-15-211134-puc-scale/rows.tsv:6`; [research/memory-accounting.md](research/memory-accounting.md):838-842). Stages A-C, THE WINDOW and the reserve's size have since changed what happens at the cap (a growth past it is lent from a bounded credit), and no results note since re-runs either. Re-run both on HEAD's natives with stock in the same pinned chain (`test/native/census-os.sh`, `bench/oc/gc-pace-sweep.sh`) and record whether each survives, dies of the deadline as PUC does, or is still refused; then restate or retire the emergency-GC row's standing divergence.
- [x] **THE COLLECTOR AT THE WALL: we refuse where PUC collects and retries, and it is not rare -- found by the capacity probe 2026-10-03** ([bench/results-ramscale-2026-10-03.md](../bench/results-ramscale-2026-10-03.md)). A program that fills memory and catches "not enough memory" fails on ours where stock recovers: 20 of our 60 capacity runs (19 fills and one boot) against 0 of 20 on stock, at OC's 1.8 on the 192, 256 and 1024 KB sticks. Two modes. *Recovery refused* (12): the program caught the refusal and dropped its data, and its own next allocation -- one short string -- was refused, because our allocator never collects (`lj52shim.c:378-381`, `:440-447`) where PUC's `luaM_realloc_` runs a full collection and retries (`lmem.c:85-93`). *Machine down* (7): a refusal was raised where no handler of the program caught it -- OpenOS's dispatcher, the kernel, or the probe's own allocations between batches -- and the machine stopped. Both persist at 1024 KB, so a larger scale moves the wall and changes nothing at it. This is the residual the emergency-GC row records as narrowed, not closed. Three more from the same runs: (1) in the last quarter of memory -- once the heap after a cycle stays above total - max(total/4, 128 KB) -- `lj52_gc_pressure` re-arms a full cycle at every checkpoint after an allocation, and a filling program's last batches run 20-120x stock's, with the JIT off as much as on; (2) a 192 KB machine whose `kernelMemory` holds no traces (the JIT switched on only after kernel init) idles 4-22 KB above the 128 KiB watermark floor and collects continuously (1390-2964 arms and 16-29 trace flushes in 10 s), and failed one boot in 12, while the shipped configuration does not, only because (3) kernel init's traces are counted in `kernelMemory`, which OC grants on top, and later flushed: a windfall of 170-250 KB, bimodal run to run (`kernelMemory` 335-413 KB against a trace-free 164 393 B), whose dependence on host speed is untested. And a code reading, not observed: an arm landing mid-sweep reaches GCSpause without atomic(), stays armed while the threshold has just moved to twice the estimate, and with the estimate above half the cap can leave the collector parked until a refusal, the kernel's tenth-resume collect or the 65 536-call bailout. The fail-first test exists: `OCLJ_PROBE=capacity` (`test/native/OcljSmoke.scala`; the matrix in [bench/runs/2026-10-03-ramscale/](../bench/runs/2026-10-03-ramscale/)). **Design, 2026-10-04** ([bench/results-wall-2026-10-04.md](../bench/results-wall-2026-10-04.md); the requirements, three designs and the judges in [bench/runs/2026-10-04-wall/design/](../bench/runs/2026-10-04-wall/design/)): in the shim only, no LuaJIT change, the collector gate kept. A bounded credit past the cap (G = total/16, 32-512 KB; G/2 before a refusal, G after one, and a small slice for the kernel), hysteresis on the re-arm, the park reset, and last, `kernelMemory` made trace-free by keeping the JIT off through kernel init. A collection carried by the watchdog hook stays a later stage, for if the credit falls short. **Status 2026-10-04: stage A, the park, done.** `lj52_gc_pressure` restarts an arm that the old cycle's end left at the pause with the threshold past `gc.total` (THE PARK RESET), and the 65 536-call valve counts growth attempts only. `mem_test` W5a-c fail on the 2026-10-03 shim (armed at the pause, stepmul 0, threshold twice the heap; a 64-byte churn refused after 3195 tables with no collection; a 100 000-block sweep read as a bailout) and pass after, each with a sabotage that fails it. In the machine the park did not occur: stage A's reduced matrix (arm E, 192 KB, 12 runs) read 6 clean, 3 recovery refused, 3 down, against chain1's 5 / 3 / 3 and one boot failure, and its park resets stayed 0. The down mode is P1, not the park. And `acc-4` had passed only because the collector was parked: it read the native figure after the refusal, and a collector that runs collects the fill first (the fill is now kept reachable). **Status 2026-10-04: stage B, the credit and the cadence, done.** A growth that would pass the cap is lent from a bounded credit (G = total/16, 32-512 KB: G/2 before the first refusal, G after it, 16 KB more for the kernel only), charged, and repaid at the next checkpoint; the allocator still never collects; every refusal arms; and the emergency cycle re-arms after half the post-cycle headroom, not at every checkpoint. `mem_test` W1-W14 were each seen failing first (on the 2026-10-03 shim, stage A's, or the two stage-B builds that had a back-off), and each mechanism has a sabotage that fails exactly its checks. The capacity matrix at 1.8 (our D/E/O, 60 runs, beside stock in the same pinned chain): no "recovery refused" and no machine down, against 19 of 60 on 2026-10-03; the last five batches 0.7-2.7x stock's in 39 of 41 cells, against 21-121x; 0.5-5.5 arms a batch, against 100-341; capacity 0.99-7.14x stock's, at least stock's in every cell but O closures at 1024 KB (8087 against 8192). The design review's back-off for pre-emptive cycles was built and removed: on both builds that had it the harness's `mem-2` found it suppressing the proofs the trace flush is asked for at. **What remains:** three stalls and one dropin machine down in 68 runs (stock 0 of 20), where the heap met a credit tier's top at an allocation outside any handler of the program's (the probe's stage string or `event.timer`, or OpenOS's dispatcher) -- stock collects at the refusal and rescues those when garbage covers them; we cannot collect in the allocator, and a collection carried by the watchdog hook would not save the refused allocation either. Not fixed; one idea, not built: lend the first crossing after each proof and let the cycle it arms decide. And one boot failure, unexplained: an assertion in OC's synchronized-call stack protocol one tick after boot with 322 KB free, the first in about 140 harness runs. **Status 2026-10-04: stage C, a trace-free `kernelMemory`, done -- and with it the row.** Kernel site 13 (`native/kernel/patch-machine-lua.lua`, THE FIFTH CHANGE) keeps the JIT off from the kernel's first line until OpenComputers has taken its memory baseline, then restores it as it was; the kernel records the traces live at the baseline (`_OCLJ_KERNEL_TRACES`, harness `km-1`: 0). `kernelMemory` reads 164 923 B on every boot of the additive build, pinned and unpinned (164 247 B on the dropin; stock 174 605 B), where it read 335-414 KB with kernel init's traces in it. A proven cycle now asks for the trace flush only inside HALF the watermark (`LJ52_GC_FLUSHSHIFT`): a 192 KB machine idled at 106-145 KB free against a 130 KB watermark and threw away ~25 KB of compiled code 8-20 times in 10 s; it now does so 0 times in 13 of 16 runs, the other three once each with 83-86 KB of traces resident. Capacity at small sticks drops to its honest figure (D at 192 KB 1.53-2.78x stock's, from up to 4.5x with the windfall), at least stock's in every cell; no boot failed. Fail-first: `build-kernel.sh`'s postflight on the 12-site patcher, `km-1` with site 13's `jit.off()` neutralised (246 traces, 339 943 B), `mem_test` W15 on stage B's shim, and a sabotage that fails exactly W15. What is left is two new rows below.
- [x] **A refusal at a credit tier's top can land outside the program's handler -- found 2026-10-04** ([bench/results-wall-2026-10-04.md](../bench/results-wall-2026-10-04.md), "what is left"). The collector at the wall lends a bounded credit past the cap and collects at the next checkpoint, but cannot collect inside the allocator; a program whose live data reaches a tier's top is refused at whichever allocation comes next. Usually that is inside the program's own `pcall`; in the capacity probe it was sometimes the probe's own code between batches (the closure handed to `pcall`, the stage string, `event.timer`) -- a stall, the machine up and the program's timer chain broken -- or OpenOS's dispatcher, where the dropin went down once. Stage B's matrix: 3 stalls and 1 dropin down in 68 runs of ours; stage C's: 1 stall in 49; stock 0 of 20 in each. Stock collects at the refusal and so rescues every crossing that garbage covers; we refuse when live data plus the garbage since the last cycle crosses. A collection carried by the watchdog hook would not save the refused allocation either. Idea, not built: lend the first crossing after each proof and let the cycle it arms decide, which moves the refusal to the next allocation after a collection. **Status 2026-10-05: narrowed by THE WINDOW; what remains is the second-chance row below** ([bench/results-wall-window-2026-10-05.md](../bench/results-wall-window-2026-10-05.md)). No garbage-covered outside-handler landing in any hermetic sweep with the JIT off; in the machine's amplified probe, bad outcomes 15 of 50 on stage C, 1-2 of 50 now.
  - **What it was.** A survey of the five bad runs found that each had a second refusal and failed at it: a first refusal absorbed outside the fill (the probe's paint, the JIT recorder) opened the reserve tier, and the reserve top's refusal landed outside a handler. The stage string was not the site; event.timer or the closure handed to `pcall` was. A hermetic reproduction (no JVM) refused outside the handler in 431 of 16 384 caps on stage C, every one garbage-covered, against 0 of 90 112 on stock PUC. Three mechanisms: a proof taken while a frame pinned its churn, a two-part object refused on its second part, and the kernel spending its slice without arming.
  - **The fix** (`native/lj52shim.c`, THE WINDOW; zero LuaJIT lines). A sandbox growth past its tier's top is lent, at most 4 KiB further, and arms the cycle that decides it. A proof back under the top shuts the window; data that survived two consecutive cycles past it is the verdict, and the next crossing is refused. A growth past the cap first proves a finished cycle; a refusal shuts the window; the kernel gets no window and keeps 12 KiB past the sandbox's ceiling (pinned at compile time). The sandbox's bound is cap + G + 4 KiB; every thread's is unchanged at cap + G + 16 KiB.
  - **Chosen by a judge panel** of three prototyped designs (lend, a re-derived verdict machine, prevention), then a code review of four lenses with a verifier per finding: 16 of 18 findings confirmed, all minor, all fixed -- among them the window's own lend tripping the fresh-record rule (W11w), and no coverage of the legacy path (the W16 family now runs there too).
  - **Measured.** mem_test 81 checks: the window's cases (the W16 family and W17) fail on stage C in every run, the kernel and fresh-record checks pass there, and all 81 pass in 30 of 30. The negative control: 35 of 35, eleven new sabotages. In the machine, an amplified capacity probe (batch 10): stage C 15 bad of 50, THE WINDOW 1 of 50 (p = 0.00009); after the review's fixes 2 of 50 (p = 0.0005). The standard matrix (batch 100) 68 of 68 clean, and its 48 runs of ours clean again after the fixes. Near the wall 1.16x stage C's time (median, same chain); capacity at least stock's in every cell but one (1024 KB closures 0.98-0.99x against a stock reading of 8 192, which read 7 313 in chain C). Judged by the gate's own pre-registered criterion, not every part passed (the results note says which).
  - **Residual.** A window overrun before a checkpoint (more than 4 KiB with no checkpoint, a single request past the top plus 4 KiB, the kernel spending more than 4 KiB of its slice past the sandbox's top) and a verdict on garbage pinned across two cycles are still refused wherever they land; in the machine with the JIT on, a refusal absorbed outside the fill still opens the reserve tier for a program that never saw it (3 downs in 30 amplified JIT-on record runs across the two builds, against 8 bad in 15 on stage C; its own row below). Stock stalls under the amplified probe too (2 of 20). **Status 2026-10-10:** the window overrun and the verdict on garbage pinned across two cycles moved to the verdict-lottery row.
- [x] **A refused allocation inside an interpreted fast function crashes the process in LuaJIT's Windows unwinder -- found 2026-10-05, root-caused, fixed the same day** ([bench/results-wall-window-2026-10-05.md](../bench/results-wall-window-2026-10-05.md), "The unwinder crash"; `bench/runs/2026-10-05-window/crash/`). `tostring` of a number (and `string.char`, `sub`, `reverse`, `lower`, `upper`) sets `L->base` but not `L->top` before its C call (`vm_x64.dasc:1385`); when our allocator refuses the result string, `lj_err_mem` skips its top refresh for a non-Lua frame (`lj_err.c:823-830`) and pushes the message onto the frame's own slot, and `err_unwind` dereferences it: exit 139, which in a machine is the whole JVM. A latent LuaJIT bug, reachable from ordinary sandbox code near the RAM cap with the JIT on; the shipped stage-C shim reached it a few times in the design round's hermetic sweeps (1 of 4 096 amplified caps in one census), though not again under verification on current binaries (layout-dependent); THE WINDOW in 0 of 18 432 (it refuses elsewhere); a rejected design in 17-43 %. Root cause and fix confirmed by an investigator and an adversarial verifier, on the rejected design's shim over the same LuaJIT. The fix is three lines in `lj_err_mem` clamping a stale top up to the frame's base (`crash/patch-fastfunc-errmem-top.sh`, in native/luajit's patch style): crashes 83 → 0 of 256 caps, mem_test unchanged. **Status 2026-10-05: fixed** ([bench/results-errmem-2026-10-05.md](../bench/results-errmem-2026-10-05.md); archive [bench/runs/2026-10-05-errmem/](../bench/runs/2026-10-05-errmem/)). `native/luajit/patch-fastfunc-errmem-top.sh` inserts the clamp (`if (L->top < L->base) L->top = L->base;`) right before the push in `lj_err_mem` and, after the code review found the second site, before the push in `lj_err_err` too -- the function `lj_err_mem` hands to inside an `xpcall` message handler (`L->status == LUA_ERRERR`, `lj_err.c:815-816`), where there is no top refresh at all. `build-native.sh` applies it to a pristine copy of `lj_err.c` on every build and platform, as it does the penalty scrub: the checkout refused if it carries any form of the clamp, the clamp asserted absent after the re-copy and present exactly twice before make, `lj_err.o` checked newer after. The checkout refusal (on this form and on the investigator's `else if` form), the present-twice assertion and the script's nine mangling refusals were shown to fire. The evidence is a census rather than a unit test -- no deterministic standalone reproducer has been written; which caps crash moves with the seed and with ASLR: on the rejected design's shim, 1 653 crashes in 4 608 caps unpatched against 0 in 4 096 patched; with ASLR off, every non-crashing cap's output identical with and without the fix (4 809 caps across three builds, 1 746 more across two) and every crashing one ending inside the batch's own `pcall`; the shipped `libluajit.a` matching the measured fix on 2 048 of 2 048 caps. The handler site is not covered by the census. Gates on the new natives (additive `d9a51b6b`, dropin `8635573e`, Linux additive `21156504`; the shim unchanged): Windows mem 81, wd 35, shim, security, race, penalty and the negative control (35 of 35) pass; Linux mem, wd and penalty pass; the five full harness suites pass. The Linux native in `dist/` had been from 2026-10-03, before the collector at the wall; it is rebuilt with this change. Worth an upstream report (the upstream-reports row). **Status 2026-10-10:** the second clamp site, in `lj_err_err` (inside an `xpcall` message handler, `L->status == LUA_ERRERR`), rests on the code reading: no census exercises it ([bench/results-errmem-2026-10-05.md](../bench/results-errmem-2026-10-05.md):188-189; `bench/runs/2026-10-05-errmem/README.md:36-37`). The upstream-reports row carries it, as the open caveat on report (3).
- [x] **A refusal the program never saw opens the reserve tier for it -- found 2026-10-05, bounded the same day by THE RESERVE'S SIZE** ([bench/results-wall-window-2026-10-05.md](../bench/results-wall-window-2026-10-05.md), "What is left"; the fix: [bench/results-reserve-2026-10-05.md](../bench/results-reserve-2026-10-05.md), archive [bench/runs/2026-10-05-reserve/](../bench/runs/2026-10-05-reserve/)). THE CREDIT's reserve tier gives a program G/2 more after a refusal so that catch, format and drop work. When the refusal is caught by code that is not the program's -- the capacity probe's `pcall(paint)`, the JIT recorder (`trace_abort` drops the error), OpenOS's error path -- the program never learns of it and keeps filling, and the reserve top's refusal (a verdict, on data that survived two cycles) can land outside any handler. Every bad in-machine outcome of stages B and C took this path. THE WINDOW cut it hermetically (2 899 to 182 of 16 384 JIT-on runs) but not away: in the amplified capacity probe with the JIT on, 21 of 30 record runs had a paint-absorbed refusal and 3 of them went down (stage C 8 bad in 15); with the JIT off it took the path in the amplified string cells (W 3 of 10, V 8 of 10), all clean; at the probe's own batch of 100, 3 of the final build's 28 JIT-on matrix runs (0 of the first build's) and 3 of 4 JIT-on 1024 KB closure runs in the timing chain reached the reserve tier, all clean. Stock has no reserve tier, so a refusal it absorbs gives the fill no more room. Needs its own design: the tier's size and when it closes are the levers, against the recovery it exists for (mem_test W8, W2b/W2c). **Status 2026-10-05: bounded.** The absorber was placed first, with an inert instrumented shim run in the machine: every refusal the program never saw is a 56-64 B closure the kernel's `machine.lua` makes on the sandbox thread inside a component call (`wrapUserdata`, `unwrapUserdata`, `checkArg`), caught by whatever `pcall` wraps that call; the JIT recorder and the string table absorbed 0 of 50. A three-design judge panel chose the reserve's size: the reserve a refusal opens is now `LJ52_GC_RSV` = 8 KiB past the heap it was refused at, sized once, clamped to [G/2, G] where it is read (`lj52_gc_rsvcredit`); a fresh record keeps G; a VM event handler gets no credit (as a finalizer); `_OCLJ_WALLSTATS` reports the tier. Zero LuaJIT lines, ~30 shim lines. The sandbox's bound after a refusal tightens from cap + G + 4 KiB to max(u, cap + G/2) + 12 KiB. Hermetically (the final object, 36 864 runs over 14 cell-and-seed rows): every non-second-chance cap byte-identical, 0 runs past either bound (3 545 before), every reserve exactly 8 192 B past the refused heap, capacity 0.996-1.000, the OpenOS-shaped probe's second-chance stalls gone at every seed. mem_test 86 checks (W20, W20L, W21, W22, W7m new; W7 tightened; W19, W7k, W16R read the tier), four of them born failing on THE WINDOW's object; the negative control 41 sabotages, six new, twelve sets re-measured. In the machine (166 runs, the previous build interleaved): the bound holds in 30 of 30 amplified runs where the previous build passes it in 21; the creep after an absorbed refusal 8.3 KB against 16.2; capacity within 1 % like for like and at least 2.6x stock's; the five suites pass; the cost bar missed in one cell by 0.02 (1.12x). **What it does not change:** the reserve top's verdict still lands wherever it falls (1 down in 20 JIT-on record runs against 0 in 20; history 3 in 46) -- the next row; and a recovery that formats while holding after a second chance is refused on every build including stock (0 of 6 stock, 2 of 6 the previous build, 3 of 10 this one), since the fill that never saw its refusal spent the reserve.
- [ ] **The reserve top's verdict lands wherever it falls -- the lottery THE RESERVE'S SIZE bounds but does not remove, 2026-10-05** ([bench/results-reserve-2026-10-05.md](../bench/results-reserve-2026-10-05.md), "What is left"). A verdict decided at a proof is executed by the next crossing, whoever makes it; after an absorbed refusal the fill continues past the reserve top window by window, the paint (or OpenOS's dispatcher) absorbing draw after draw, until one lands in code with no handler -- the gate's down landed in OpenOS's `init.lua` after three absorbed draws. Two levers were designed and deferred on evidence: THE ADDRESS (a handler-of-record vote by frame walking; the best hermetic record of the round, rejected as a base for a vote without decay, a trace-inlined `pcall` invisible to the walk, an unverified walk in the allocator, and a `rawverdict` control that catches nothing -- `bench/runs/2026-10-05-reserve/design/verdict.txt`, `j3-safety.txt`), and the recorder rule (a refusal inside the trace recorder's protected call opens nothing; exact, proven both ways, `design/identify/identify.diff`; its trigger -- a recorder-absorbed refusal in a machine -- fired once in the gate, a 900 B trace copy in a JIT-on recovery). The address's decisive input, which handler owns most past-cap allocations in a machine, is still to be measured with the per-allocation tracer. A silent past-cap cadence (−11 % cycles hermetically) is the cost follow-up. **Status 2026-10-10:** Takes over THE WINDOW's residual, which only the ticked tier's-top row recorded: a window overrun before a checkpoint (more than 4 KiB with no checkpoint, a single request past the top plus 4 KiB, or the kernel spending more than 4 KiB of its slice past the sandbox's top, whose refusal lands on the dispatcher's `table.pack`) and a verdict on garbage pinned across two cycles are still refused wherever they land (`lj52shim.c:1123-1128`; `bench/results-wall-window-2026-10-05.md:463-469`; `bench/results-reserve-2026-10-05.md:356-357`; hermetically 725 downs in 2 048 kernel-garbage string caps, `:212-214`). For the user: after a second chance, a recovery that formats while holding is refused at its first allocation (F 7 of 7 such runs) and stock, refused with its live data at the cap, recovers in none (0 of 6); accept it as parity with stock, or keep it open here (`bench/results-reserve-2026-10-05.md:303-317`). A refused recorder allocation, unseen by the program, still penalises its loop, and repeated refusals at one loop blacklist it to the interpreter for its prototype's life (`lj_trace.c:604-615`, `penalty_pc` `:392-414`); a residual in `bench/runs/2026-10-05-reserve/design/d3-final.txt:645-647`, unmeasured in a machine. The tracer run is that file's M6 (`:607-612`), which has not run. The silent past-cap cadence named just before this note moved on 2026-10-10 to the past-cap costs row. Also open here: the reserve's 8 KiB is a margin, not a measurement; no recovery that fits between 4 and 8 KiB was measured (`bench/results-reserve-2026-10-05.md:368-370`; `bench/runs/2026-10-05-reserve/design/d3-final.txt:661-663`).
- [ ] **Fail-first gaps: the verdict read against the current top, and mem-2's near-empty clause -- found 2026-10-03 and 2026-10-05, added 2026-10-10.** (1) THE WINDOW refuses a crossing on the verdict only while the deciding proof's heap stays past the current top, so that a cap raised, or frees that took `gc_low` back under the top, between the proof and the crossing void it (`native/lj52shim.c:1077-1083`, the check at `:1286`). No test reaches that case, because the two are usually one allocator call apart ([bench/results-wall-window-2026-10-05.md](../bench/results-wall-window-2026-10-05.md):447-450). Wanted: a mem_test case that puts one between them, and a sabotage that reads the verdict against the proof's top, seen to fail it. (2) `mem-2-pressure-flushes-traces`'s near-empty clause (traces_live at most max(2, 5 % of the count before) while the program runs) was checked only against two sabotaged runs' logged values, which fail its first clause too, with 0 flushes ([bench/results-ramscale-2026-10-03.md](../bench/results-ramscale-2026-10-03.md):212-217; `test/native/OcljSmoke.scala:433-445`); the mem-2 failures since had no flush either (the stage-B back-off builds, [bench/results-wall-2026-10-04.md](../bench/results-wall-2026-10-04.md):281-306). Wanted: a sabotaged native whose pressure flush runs but leaves traces live, failing mem-2 on that clause alone. Same class as the 10-02 loose-ends row's (4), the `LUAJIT_USE_SYSMALLOC` refusal never seen to fail.
- [ ] **The wall's past-cap costs: idle under a tier's top, the never-verdict band, and the silent cadence -- found 2026-10-05, added 2026-10-10.** (1) **Idle past the cap.** Hermetically, with live data 15 KB past the cap (1 KB under the burst top) and 50 garbage strings a resume, stage C runs 12 475 cycles in 3 000 resumes and THE WINDOW 13 604, about a full cycle per 500 B of garbage; idle below the cap is unchanged ([bench/results-wall-window-2026-10-05.md](../bench/results-wall-window-2026-10-05.md):484; `bench/runs/2026-10-05-window/design/j2-practice.md:196-203`, `:515-516`; `d2-final.md:795-797`, and `:1149`, "its own row"). Not re-run on HEAD's shim, and never in a machine: `CAP-IDLE` idles before the fill (`test/native/OcljSmoke.scala:3373-3386`). (2) **The never-verdict band.** Live data past a tier's top by less than its own garbage per cycle is lent at one full cycle per crossing and never refused (results-wall-window-2026-10-05.md:478-480; K2: 2 000 cycles in 2 000 iterations, `d2-final.md:811-814`), which is stock's rate: stock collects at every allocation that does not fit. A decision: accept it as parity with stock, or measure a verdict after N = 4 and N = 8 consecutive open proofs (N = 2 made W16 worse; `d2-final.md:1129-1135`). (3) **The silent past-cap cadence**, taken over from the verdict-lottery row: in d2-verdict's design it measured −11 % cycles near the wall, where THE WINDOW costs 1.16x stage C's time (results-wall-window-2026-10-05.md:482-483), and 3 055 cycles in the idle case above (`j2-practice.md:196-203`); its risk is a heap that reaches the top fuller of garbage, so that a checkpoint-free stretch longer than the 4 KiB window is refused more often (`d2-final.md:1137-1140`). Not built.
- [x] **One boot died on OpenComputers' own synchronized-call assertion -- found 2026-10-04; the harness, fixed the same day** ([bench/results-wall-2026-10-04.md](../bench/results-wall-2026-10-04.md), "The boot that died on OC's assertion"). `one-array-r2-D-B3` (stage B's capacity matrix), on the boot loop's first tick with 322 843 B free: `NativeLuaArchitecture.runSynchronized` asserts `lua.getTop == 2` (`:172`; the kernel thread and the function to call, guaranteed by `run()`), the stack held something else, OC logged "Faulty architecture implementation for synchronized calls" and the machine stopped with `Error.InternalError`. No refusal is visible. The only one in about 220 harness runs that day. A lead not followed: in 76 of that chain's 93 runs the harness's raw-state reads logged a dirty stack (`getTop=2`, expected 1), i.e. they read the state while a synchronized call was pending -- the harness may be what disturbs it, or our native may leave a value behind; a targeted repro followed (below). **Status 2026-10-04: the harness, not the mod.**
  - **The cause.** `guard()`'s six VM-fingerprint reads ran on the machine's raw state without the machine's monitor. Along with f6's and gc-pace's re-applies after a restore, they were the last unlocked reads touching a live machine's stack. `quiesced()` before them proves only that the machine is not executing at that moment. Each `evalStr` is several jnlua calls ending in a `setTop` to the top it found, so a resume the worker ran in between lost what it pushed, or got nils added.
  - **The probes.** New mode `OCLJ_PROBE=rawrace`: the fingerprint's reads beside the running machine, `race-1` after the boot and `race-2` (`OCLJ_RACE_PHASE=boot`) through it.
    - **Unlocked:** the machine wedged in `Sleep(Int.MaxValue)` with the kernel's sleep value gone, or OC stopped it on its own asserts (`runThreaded`'s `:195`, `:200` and `:257`, and, through the boot, `runSynchronized`'s `:172` itself: an exact reproduction of the original failure, same assertion and stack). Of 11 unlocked `race-1` runs, the 9 that were measured all broke; one of them passed the first heartbeat bar, which was then raised. Through the boot, 10 of 16 unlocked runs broke.
    - **Locked:** 13 of 13 `race-1` runs (about 1.3 million reads each) and 6 of 6 `race-2` boots passed.
  - **The fix.** Guard, f6 and gc-pace read under the monitor (`OcljSmoke.scala`). The full suite passed after it on both arms, JIT on and off.
  - **The dirty-stack lead was OC's own shape.** Under the monitor, `getTop=2` is a synchronized call between its halves. The warning now prints the slots' types, and every one since reads `[1]=THREAD [2]=FUNCTION`.
  - **A second harness race, found by the same regression run.** Stage C's kernel site 13 restores the JIT at the kernel's first resume after the baseline. When that resume had not run by the harness's JIT-off read-out (0-spin quiesces), it undid the harness's `jit.off()`. `j0` failed in both such runs and passed in all 9 others. The read-out now also makes `jit.on` a no-op in that state, which covers the dropin as well; `bench/oc/matrix.sh` and `gc-pace-sweep.sh` run the dropin with the JIT off as cell B. After it, 12 of 12 JIT-off suites passed on the two arms, one of them a 0-spin boot.
  - **Unaffected.** Arm O switched the JIT off before kernel init all along, so its data stands. The mod, the native and the kernel are unchanged.
- [x] **Migration hazard: `kernelMemory` is persisted to NBT** (`NativeLuaArchitecture.save`/`load`). A world written by a build with accounting dead carries `kernelMemory == 1`; loading it under this build recomputes `totalMemory` as `1 + ram` and starves the machine. No such save exists yet — nothing has run in Minecraft — but this must not reach an existing world without a migration that discards a `kernelMemory` of 1. **Superseded -- 2026-10-03:** the world this row guards against was never written. The accounting landed in 410b3ee (2026-09-03), thirteen days before anything ran in Minecraft (first in-game run 6ca53ac, 2026-09-16), so every build that reached a world postdates it; the migration was not built, and `LuaJITArchitecture.java:614` reads the NBT value unguarded. The forward path is asserted instead: harness `f5-restore-memory-still-accounted` (`OcljSmoke.scala:5442`) requires `kernelMemory > 10000` after a real persist and restore. By reading, the one way left to report a 1 is the unaccounted `luaL_newstate` fallback of the 10-02 loose ends below (no record, so `ocljUsedMemory` answers -1 and jnlua's own figure never moves).
- [x] **Assert the deadline watchdog still fires, now that allocation behaviour changed under it.** A sandbox `pcall(function() while true do end end)` must still produce `too long without yielding`. The watchdog is the highest-consequence untested path in the system, it shares the hook slot the deadline-hook row is about, and enforcing the RAM cap changes what happens underneath it. Two lines of Lua in the harness's autorun chunk. **Done -- verified 2026-10-03:** The harness's autorun runs `pcall(function() while true do end end)` (`OcljSmoke.scala:2652`). `k1-deadline-still-fires` (:4548) requires `too_long_without_yielding` and `k5-watchdog-fired` (:4597) requires the shim's fire count >= 1. Both PASS in `bench/runs/2026-09-16-additive-smoke/milestones.txt` and in the 10-03 accounting change's additive harness run, 65/0 ([bench/results-accounting-2026-10-03.md](../bench/results-accounting-2026-10-03.md)). A caught timeout that outlives its grace is `k6-grace-expiry-crashes-cleanly` (`OCLJ_PROBE=grace`) and in-game T4.
- [ ] **One bare-frame allocation is still uncovered, and it is worth a decision rather than a shrug.** `throw()` (`jnlua.c:2353-2366`, the call at `:2362`) calls `lua_tostring` in a bare JNI frame when `throw_protected` itself failed; stringifying a NON-string error value allocates. It cannot bite on the out-of-memory path, because LuaJIT preallocates and GC-fixes the "not enough memory" message (`lj_state.c:202`) — only on something like sandbox `error(42)` raised exactly at the wall. Narrow, but it is the one hole the pushcfunction window does not close. **Corrected 2026-10-03:** plain `error(42)` is not the trigger. `throw()` runs `throw_protected` under a `lua_pcall`, and that stringifies the value inside the pcall (`jnlua.c:2333`, through the shim's `luaL_tolstring`), where running out of memory leaves only the preallocated message, a string. The hole needs `throw_protected` itself to fail with a NON-string error, for instance an error object whose `__tostring` raises a number, at the wall. The comment at `lj52shim.c:2234-2240` repeats the old range and example. **Status 2026-10-10:** Still open, a decision for the user: cover it or accept it in writing. Covering it takes either a second patch step on jnlua.c, which is built today with exactly one logic line patched (`build-native.sh:639-649`), or a header-level wrapper that runs every `lua_tolstring` in jnlua under `norefuse`, as `lua_pushcfunction` already runs (`lj52shim.h:325`, `lj52shim.c:2241-2246`). "At the wall" predates THE CREDIT (f21a64b): a kernel-class allocation, which includes a bare frame between resumes (`wd_depth` 0), may go 16 KiB past the tier's top (`LJ52_GC_KSLICE`; `lj52_gc_kernel`, `lj52_gc_credit`, `lj52shim.c:1234-1252`), by reading. Reachability from sandbox code is not checked.
- [x] **Benchmark the accounting's cost.** A JNI field get and set on every allocation. Stock OC pays the same on PUC Lua so it is not a regression against OC, but it IS one against our previous unlimited build, and this project sells speed. Batching the publish would trade `freeMemory` precision for throughput; do not do it without a measurement. **Half measured 2026-09-22** ([bench/results-ladder-2026-09-22.md](../bench/results-ladder-2026-09-22.md), the sandbox-tax ladder): the shim's own bookkeeping in `lj52_alloc` — the get, the delta arithmetic, `lj52_gc_pressure`, the set, on every allocation — costs 0.89–1.07x with the JIT on and 0.93–1.10x off (min of 5), 1.05/1.10 on binarytrees' 14.08 M accounted calls. That is with plain C accessors in a bare host; the JNI field get/set this row names is still inside the unseparated in-machine residual (the sandbox + JVM residual row), so the row stays open for exactly that part. **Re-measured 2026-09-29 on the new box, idle** ([bench/results-ladder-2026-09-29.md](../bench/results-ladder-2026-09-29.md) section 3): binding the accounting reads 0.97–1.14x on the seven benchmarks with resolvable times (0.98–1.12x with the compiler on, 0.97–1.14x off), 1.12–1.14x on binarytrees — at most 3–5 ns per accounted call, and a bound rather than a measurement: two resolved pairs have the accounting arm FASTER (nqueens 0.990, sieve interpreted 0.966), so heap layout and the trace tree move with the arm too. The JNI crossing is still unmeasured; the row stays open. **On the arena, 2026-10-02** ([bench/results-allocator-2026-10-02.md](../bench/results-allocator-2026-10-02.md)): with each state's blocks in its own `lj_alloc` arena (the allocator row), the accounting reads 1.134x on binarytrees standalone (the arena host with the 64 MB accounting against the same host without it, r3l/r3ul, min of 5 fresh processes), strings2 1.039x, matmul 1.024x, sieve 1.012x — 0.028 s over binarytrees' 14.08 M accounted calls, at most about 2 ns each (an upper bound on a mixture, as on 09-29). Still with plain C accessors; the JNI crossing is still unmeasured, and the row stays open. **With the allocator rung gone, 2026-10-03** ([bench/results-allocator-2026-10-02.md](../bench/results-allocator-2026-10-02.md#in-game-t8-2026-10-03), T8): binarytrees' in-machine cost over the bare host is about 0.15–0.18 s a run on both DLLs against cold host processes — the game 0.153 s above the C-library host at T7 (0.5586 against 0.4060) and 0.183 s above the arena host with the accounting at T8 (0.4202 against 0.2370); against the C-library host's later fresh loads in one process (0.339–0.375 s, a first-run step the long-lived machine may already have paid) T7's would be 0.18–0.22 s — about 11–13 ns per accounted call (up to 16 on that reading) if spread over the 14.08 M calls (a per-call equivalent, not a per-call measurement). mandelbrot, under 800 calls a run, shows none of it (0.980x the arena host); in game matmul comes to 11.8 ns a call over its 0.82 M calls, against binarytrees' 13.0; sieve, in the pinned harness, to about 210 ns over its 72.5 K, in a machine whose emergency collector runs 640–800 times, which one per-call cost does not explain. Candidates, none separated and none ranked: the JNI crossing — jnlua's `getthreadenv` + `getluamemory` + `setluamemory` on every allocation, where the bare host has plain C accessors; the machine's arena, long-lived and already used by OpenOS's boot and the earlier benchmarks, against each host process's fresh one; collections over a state that also holds OpenOS and the kernel; the sandbox; the JVM. The JNI crossing is still unmeasured. The next arm to build: a host on jnlua's real JNI accessors under a JVM, or a batched-publish prototype (measured, as this row asks, before anything ships). The row stays open. **Measured 2026-10-03** ([bench/results-allocator-2026-10-02.md](../bench/results-allocator-2026-10-02.md#what-the-machine-still-pays-per-allocation-the-jni-crossing-2026-10-03)): the JVM arm, and two of the other candidates. **The JNI crossing:** the shipping additive DLL (`88b50796`) loaded with `System.load` into a plain JVM (Temurin 8.0.504; no OC, no OpenOS, no sandbox) and driven through our `LuaStateLuaJIT` — `new LuaStateLuaJIT(67108864)`, so jnlua's capped allocator turns the shim's accounting on and every allocation calls `getthreadenv` + `getluamemory` + `setluamemory` through JNI, against `new LuaStateLuaJIT()`, accounting off; `heap = 1` in both; binarytrees loaded afresh 5 times, 3 JVMs per arm, launched with the performance-core mask (neither chain read the affinity back). Accounting on 0.389–0.399 s, off 0.209–0.222 s: 0.180 s a run at the mins, 12.8 ns per accounted call over the 14.08 M (11.9–13.5 across the reps). The same pair in the bare host on plain C accessors is 0.022–0.031 s (about 2 ns a call), so the crossing itself costs about 0.15 s a run, 10.6 ns a call (0.149 s against the matched no-ballast, no-prewarm cell at the mins; 0.117–0.172 s, 8.3–12.2 ns, with both pairs' spreads in that cell). With the accounting off the JVM reads like the bare host (0.209–0.222 against 0.206–0.219 s). **Ballast and prewarm** (the bare host relinked against the shipping shim object, and `luajit.exe`; 2 processes per cell): a live ballast of 400 KB held through the run (about a machine's resident heap: b2 read `used` 339 184–444 925 B, 331–434 KiB in the ballast's unit, across the 10-02 chain runs) and mandelbrot and matmul run five times each first (the in-game runner's order) move binarytrees 0.983–1.019x, every pair of cells overlapping; 1200 KB costs 1.017–1.068x — so collections over a resident heap of OpenOS's size and an arena already used, as reproduced, are at most about 0.004 s of the remainder. **Conclusion:** the per-allocation JNI crossing this row names is measured, at about 11 ns a call over plain C accessors, and in a plain JVM it reproduces 0.152 s of binarytrees' 0.183 s in-machine remainder at T8: with it binarytrees reads 0.389–0.399 s, 0.024–0.041 s under the machine's mins (pinned harness 0.4127/0.4295, T8 0.4202). That a machine pays the same is inferred from that agreement, not measured in a machine; the remaining 0.02–0.04 s (sandbox, OpenOS, the machine's executor) is unseparated. One arm per candidate, one benchmark, n = 3 JVMs. **Proposed next step, not built:** stop crossing JNI on every allocation without losing `freeMemory` precision anywhere a program or OC can observe it. (Prototypes measured 2026-10-03, scratch only, in the JVM arm: caching the reads 0.354-0.383 s, plus lazy publishing 0.296-0.303 s, plus a lazy `getthreadenv` 0.250-0.259 s, against 0.385-0.397 s shipping and 0.208-0.218 s with the accounting off; every cached variant fails `mem_test` -- a stale `total` misses Java's cap changes, a lazy `used` is up to 1 KB stale -- which is why the boundaries below are needed; the results doc's JNI section has the table.) Keep `used` and `total` in the shim's per-state record (`lj52_mem`), so `lj52_alloc`'s delta, refusal and `lj52_gc_pressure` touch only C fields; publish `used` into jnlua's `luaMemoryUsed` at the points Java can read it — when a `lua_*` call returns to Java, and before a Java function is invoked from Lua — and read `luaMemoryTotal` back at those same boundaries, since Java's `setTotalMemory` writes it. By the source, jnlua's `resume`, `call`, `getFreeMemory`, `getTotalMemory` and `setTotalMemory` are all `synchronized` on the `LuaState`, so another thread going through them cannot read the fields mid-call; that is what makes the boundaries enumerable. Open design questions: every Lua→Java entry (jnlua's `calljavafunction`, and any other path that runs Java code under Lua) and every Java→Lua return must sync, or `freeMemory` goes stale where a program can see it (`computer.freeMemory()` is itself a Java call from Lua); whether any reader bypasses the synchronized accessors (not audited); how the shim gets control at each boundary, when today it reaches `jnlua.c` through macros in `lj52shim.h` that replace some of its `lua_*` calls (`lua_resume`, `lua_close`, `lua_pushcfunction` among them) and has nothing at `calljavafunction`; and what a publish costs at the rate a program calls into Java. Measure the prototype before it ships, as this row asks: binarytrees in the JVM arm and in a machine, the latter being the in-machine arm this measurement lacks. The row stays open. **BUILT 2026-10-03 (6edc5e0; in game, T9, 68f1196)** ([bench/results-accounting-2026-10-03.md](../bench/results-accounting-2026-10-03.md); design [accounting-sync.md](accounting-sync.md)). Not the per-boundary sync proposed above: jnlua's `getTotalMemory`, `setTotalMemory` and `getFreeMemory` turned out to be the ONLY readers and writers of the two fields (in OC-JNLua's source, in the installed GTNH jar by javap, and in every caller: all `invokevirtual`, no reflection), so `LuaStateLuaJIT` overrides them -- `synchronized`, emitted by its generator -- and the shim keeps the figures in its record: the capped constructor and `setTotalMemory` hand the cap down, `getFreeMemory` reads the used figure up, and every allocation after that is plain C.
