Builds at the pin are unchanged: on Windows `eris_lj.o`, the shim, jnlua and all 77 LuaJIT objects equal `build/native`'s, and AlmaLinux 8 gives the release .so files byte for byte [M: scratch `claude-tmp/oclj/pin/impl`]. Moving the pin: [jit-guide.md](jit-guide.md) 8.2a. ~~Still open from this row: the build copy `luajit-$PLATFORM` is still made only when absent (`build-native.sh:671-676`), and the pin check reads the checkout, not the copy, so after a bump an `OCLJ_BUILD` whose copy came from the older checkout builds every file but the re-copied ones from the old tree, under a fingerprint that names the new commit; 8.2a rebuilds in a fresh `OCLJ_BUILD` for that reason [S][I].~~ **Closed 2026-10-20, the build-copy round.** The review measured two holes, worse than the bump case: after an `OCLJ_LUAJIT_UNPINNED=1` build of `8c23d6aa`, a pinned build in the same `OCLJ_BUILD` compiled the parent's `lj_opt_mem.o` (`05d34fc1`, not the pin's `42bd49f9`), and a checkout at the pin with that file left uncommitted did the same; both DLLs were stamped `1ee778a4` and gradle accepted them [M]. Now a pinned build needs git, refuses a HEAD that is not the pin's 40 digits or whose commit is not in the checkout's `.git`, and remakes the build copy on every run from the pin's tree exported with `git archive <pin>^{tree}` (the tree, because archiving the commit substitutes `.relver` and turns `LuaJIT 2.1.ROLLING` into `LuaJIT 2.1.1787165859`, changing four LuaJIT objects and the fingerprint [M]); the checkout's working tree only feeds a WARNING naming what differs, which is not built. `OCLJ_LUAJIT_UNPINNED=1` builds the working tree, stamps `unpinned` and is not collected into `dist`; gradle refuses it [S][M]. Both holes now build `42bd49f9` (HEAD's script: `05d34fc1`); a `.git/HEAD` forged to `1ee778a4` plus 32 zeros and a HEAD naming a missing commit are refused; a pinned build without git is refused on Windows and in AlmaLinux 8; canonical builds equal `build/native`'s, 77 LuaJIT objects and the three of ours, and AlmaLinux 8 gives the release .so files byte for byte (`6c888050`, `6c0715d1`) from a build copy that is now all LF [M: scratch `claude-tmp/oclj/pin2/impl`]. negctl-bitop's setup, canon and the SARCONST arm pass on the new build [M]. [jit-guide.md](jit-guide.md) 7.6 and P44 have the details. **Export check, 2026-10-20.** That round's review still shipped the parent's code stamped `1ee778a4`, gradle accepting it, through the export itself: a replace ref of the pin by its parent, a smudge filter (from `.git/info/attributes` and the repo's config, or from the environment alone) that `git archive` applied while the WARNING named the wrong side, and a forged loose blob or subtree object [M]. Now every git call of a pinned build ignores replace refs and archives with no line-ending conversion, and the export is checked link by link: the pin's commit object must hash to the pin, the root tree must be the one it names, every tree object must hash to its name, and every exported file must hash to the blob id its tree gives it, the export holding exactly the tree's files. So the source tree a pinned build starts from is exactly the tree the pin's 40 digits name, up to a SHA-1 collision (round 4, below, narrowed what this says about the native); the replace ref builds the pin, and the filters, the forged objects and `export-ignore` (in the tree or in `.git/info/attributes`) are each a BUILD FAIL naming the file or tree [M]. The same round fixed three unpinned-mode hazards the review measured: a symlinked `OCLJ_LUAJIT` whose `.git` the copy's cleanup deleted in AlmaLinux 8 (the copy is now a fresh directory the checkout's contents go into, and a symlink in it is refused), an `OCLJ_LUAJIT` at the build copy's own path, deleted before it was copied (refused before any removal, as is an `OCLJ_BUILD` inside the checkout; the comparison was by spelling, A-Z folded on Windows, until round 4, below), and an `OCLJ_OUT` set to `dist`, which replaced the release library (refused in both modes) [M]. negctl-bitop.sh and negctl-pack.sh take their pristine files from the build's export when it exists, so an uncommitted line in the checkout no longer fails their setup [M]. Canonical builds equal `build/native`'s on Windows, and AlmaLinux 8 gives both release .so files byte for byte; gradle accepts the pinned DLL and refuses the unpinned one [M: scratch `claude-tmp/oclj/pin3/impl`, on build-native.sh `fc4e465d`]. **Round 4, 2026-10-20.** The export check's review measured three more holes on `fc4e465d` [M]: the directory refusals were compared by spelling, so an `OCLJ_BUILD` written `<dir>/<junction>/..` and a non-ASCII letter in other case (`Bäu`, `BÄU`) got past them, and the build deleted a throwaway checkout at the build copy's real path and replaced `dist`'s DLL with one stamped `unpinned`; and a `CFLAGS='-include <header>'` in the environment reached 76 of the 77 LuaJIT compile lines of a pinned build, which was stamped `1ee778a4`, collected into `dist` and accepted by gradle, because LuaJIT's Makefile takes its flag variables from the environment. It also read in the source that an `OCLJ_BUILD` inside the repository would overwrite tracked files of the names the build writes (`serializer/tests/strpack.lua`) [S]. Now the guards resolve every path with `cd -P` and compare directories by identity (`test -ef`, which held in Git Bash, AlmaLinux 8's sh and Ubuntu's dash), with a spelling compared, A-Z folded, only where a directory does not exist yet; the script unsets the variables of a list, those make, gcc or ld were found to take from the environment to change what is compiled or linked (the flag conventions, every variable the pin's Makefiles use without assigning it, make's own, gcc's, `LD_RUN_PATH` and `GNUTARGET`), whatever the case of the name, and names the ones it dropped; and an `OCLJ_BUILD` inside the repository is refused unless it is under `build/` [S][M]. So what is claimed is what is checked: the LuaJIT source compiled is the pin's tree (verified by hash), changed by the twelve patch scripts, compiled with build-native.sh's flags; the environment's flags on its list are dropped; the toolchain is not pinned (the release DLL is built with MinGW-w64 gcc 16.2.0, the .so with AlmaLinux 8's gcc 8.5.0). The threat model, written down: these checks guard against accidents and plausible configurations (a wrong or stale checkout or build copy, local edits, git configuration and attributes, environment variables a developer may have set, path spellings, symlinks and junctions), and the build removes or overwrites only its own products (this round's review measured four ways past that on Windows, and a variable the list missed; round 5, below, closes them and narrows both claims); deliberate tampering by someone who can edit build-native.sh itself is out of scope, and reported as low. The review's bypasses are refused with the throwaway and `dist` intact, the injected environment builds the canonical objects, canonical builds are unchanged on Windows and in AlmaLinux 8 (both release .so files byte for byte), and gradle accepts the pinned DLL and refuses the unpinned one [M: scratch `claude-tmp/oclj/pin4/impl`, and `claude-tmp/oclj/pin4/final/final-check.sh`, one chain for all of it, run into `pin4/final/run-impl/` on build-native.sh `7c3c2683`]. [jit-guide.md](jit-guide.md) 1.1 and 7.6 have the details. **Round 5, 2026-10-20.** Round 4's review measured on Windows that its guards and Windows' own programs read one spelling two ways: `cd -P` and `test -ef` take `<junction>/..` where the junction points and keep a name ending in `.` or ` `, while mingw32-make, gcc, ld and git take `..` by the spelling and drop the dot [M]. So, on `7c3c2683`, an unpinned build replaced `dist`'s pinned DLL with one stamped `unpinned` through `OCLJ_OUT=<B>/<junction>/../dist` (a regression: `fc4e465d` had refused it) and through `<B>/dist.`, also in a scratch repository's `build/native/dist`; a pinned build ran `make clean` and the LuaJIT build inside a throwaway clone through `OCLJ_BUILD=<x>/<junction>/..`; `MAKEOVERRIDES=CFLAGS=-O0` put `-O0` on 76 compile lines under mingw32-make 4.4.1, and the DLL was stamped `1ee778a4`, collected and accepted by gradle; and an `OCLJ_BUILD`/`OCLJ_OUT` pair in non-ASCII other case, neither made yet, let an unpinned DLL into the new `dist` [M: scratch `claude-tmp/oclj/pin4/verify/runs/`]. So round 4's "removes or overwrites only its own products", "paths spelled through symlinks, junctions or another letter case" and "every variable make or gcc reads" did not hold. Now every directory the script is given (`OCLJ_REPO` and its own, `OCLJ_LUAJIT`, `OCLJ_SHIM`, `OCLJ_SER`, `OCLJ_JNLUA`, `OCLJ_JNI`, `OCLJ_BUILD`, `OCLJ_OUT`) is taken at its physical path once, before anything reads it, so the guards and every tool read one path; on Windows a path with a name ending in a dot or a space is refused; the spelling comparison of directories not made yet reads bytes outside printable ASCII as `?`; `MAKEOVERRIDES` is dropped; and the repository the script lives in is guarded as `OCLJ_REPO` is [S]. The claims now say what is checked: the drop list is what was found, not every name make reads, and the build removes and overwrites only its own products within the guards' model, with the residuals in [jit-guide.md](jit-guide.md) open question 15. On build-native.sh `47b9c52e`, `claude-tmp/oclj/pin5/final/final-check2.sh` (round 4's chain, plus T1, T2, T4, U2L, `MAKEOVERRIDES`, a trailing-dot `OCLJ_SHIM` and `OCLJ_SER`, the script's own repository, `dist` checked by md5, and AlmaLinux 8 replays of U2, U3, G3 and T4) gives 79 PASS, 0 FAIL in 555 s into `pin5/final/run-impl/`, canonical builds unchanged on Windows and in AlmaLinux 8 (both release .so files byte for byte); on `7c3c2683` it gives 65 PASS and 14 FAIL (`pin5/ff-7c3c/`), the 14 being the lines for what round 5 adds (T1, T2, T4 and its AlmaLinux 8 replay, U2L, `MAKEOVERRIDES`, the trailing-dot `OCLJ_SHIM` and `OCLJ_SER`, the script's own repository), while round 4's lines and the AlmaLinux 8 replays of its refusals pass [M]. The main session ran the same chain on the final text into a fresh directory: 79 PASS, 0 FAIL in 528 s [M]; that run, the fail-first run, the chain scripts, the five rounds' reports and the claims table (every guarantee sentence with the check behind it) are archived in [bench/runs/2026-10-20-pin](../bench/runs/2026-10-20-pin/README.md). The native's "(Expected on CI.)" branch is now `build.gradle.kts:123-128`, and the kernel's is `:375-381`; by 3adfeaf the 2026-10-13 cite `:186-192` had already moved to `:210-216` [S]. The glibc row's floor check, like the fingerprint and export checks, reads only the natives that are present (`:251`), so a tag build with none still passes [S]. It adds one constraint on a release path: the Linux .so must come from a glibc 2.28 host (AlmaLinux 8), or gradle refuses it. Still open as written.
- [ ] **Sandbox constraints** — cheaper than serializer features, and several are one-liners. Do not expose `ffi` or `string.buffer` ([ffi-decision.md](ffi-decision.md)); do not expose `newproxy`; ~~make `component.list` return `next, list, nil` and restructure `componentProxy.__pairs` so neither keeps a hash-order cursor in a closure upvalue~~ **done 2026-09-19 as kernel sites 10–11, in the snapshot-walk form rather than the `next, list, nil` one: `component.list` must stay a callable table because callers do `component.list("gpu")()`** (measured: 11/12 pads diverge before, 12/12 exact after — see [forin-iterator-gap.md](forin-iterator-gap.md)); put the pure-Lua `gmatch` on the sandbox unconditionally, or set `SHORT_STRING` to 0. Full ranked list in [research/os-shape-census.md](research/os-shape-census.md). **Status 2026-10-03:** `ffi`, `string.buffer` and `newproxy` are closed by construction, with no test. `openLibs` opens no FFI and no PACKAGE library (LuaJITStateFactory.java:98-107), so `string.buffer` exists only as a registry `_PRELOAD` entry (lib_string.c:685-686) that no sandbox `require` or `debug.getregistry` can reach. `newproxy` and `jit` are kernel globals that the whitelist sandbox (build/native/kernel/machine.lua:778-1088) never copies. No harness milestone or security test asserts any of them absent. gmatch is still open: `SHORT_STRING = 500` (machine.lua:138) still sends strings under 500 bytes to native `string.gmatch` (:599-600). Its iterator is a runtime C closure that the serializer refuses by value (serializer/eris_lj.c:1697), so whether a yield inside `for w in s:gmatch(p)` saves depends on `#s`. **Status 2026-10-10:** the short-string `gmatch` item: Merged 2026-10-10 into the builtin-upvalue sweep row, which shares its milestone shape. Left here: the absence test, still unwritten (grep of OcljSmoke.scala, security_test.c and shim_test.c, 2026-10-10). **Status 2026-10-16:** `ffi` is compiled out and tested. "Closed by construction" above held for the `ffi` name, not for cdata: the lexer opened FFI for a `1LL` in sandboxed code. Now the library has no FFI (`native/luajit/oclj_features.h`), and `security_test.c` section FF (`1LL`, `2ULL`, `0x10ULL`, `3i` and `1LL << 40` are syntax errors, an FFI-flagged dump is refused, `_LOADED.ffi` and `_PRELOAD.ffi` stay nil), section BF (`string.buffer`'s FFI-only methods are gone; its objects are userdata), harness `d3-sandbox-ffi-literal-refused` and `build-native.sh`'s FFI gate all fail if it comes back. `string.buffer` and `newproxy` remain closed by construction and untested. The `eris_lj.c:1697` above is now `:1771`. **Status 2026-10-17:** the sandbox's string surface grew by three C functions, `string.pack`, `string.unpack` and `string.packsize` (the string.pack row under v1): the first new C code since the shim that parses input straight from player programs. They live in the host string table, which is the string metatable's `__index`, so `('fmt'):pack(...)` reaches them whatever `machine.lua:930-949` copies, and they run without hooks ([research/wd-blind-spots.md](research/wd-blind-spots.md) 1g): their memory is bounded by the RAM cap, but pack's time is not bounded by its output, since it reads every string argument given to a numeric option in both of its walks, so one call given a long numeric string about 8000 times through `table.unpack` stays in C for seconds (7.0-7.8 s for a 10^6-byte string in four runs, 2026-10-17, unpinned), a linear class it shares with `string.format`, `math.max` and every library function that converts a numeric string, with the hard abort as the backstop. Every read is behind 5.3.6's own overflow-safe bounds check, the one check 5.3.6 gets wrong (an `s[n]` length that wraps) is rewritten so it cannot wrap while raising 5.3.6's text, and a result above 512 bytes is one exact-size block the cap charges before anything is written (one of 512 bytes or less is built in a C array, and only the final string is charged). Tests: `test/native/run-pack.sh` (conformance, provenance against PUC 5.3.6, the shim path and the cap), `test/native/run-packfuzz.sh` (differential, against PUC 5.3.6), and `test/native/negctl-pack.sh` (28 sabotages, each caught). The `lib_string.c:685-686` above is the pinned checkout's; in the build copy it is `:707-708`, 22 lines lower. The `LuaJITStateFactory.java:98-107` above is now `:112-121`.
- [x] **LuaJIT is built without FFI, from one feature header -- decided 2026-10-13, built 2026-10-16.** With FFI compiled in, LuaJIT's lexer made cdata from `1LL`, `2ULL`, `0x10ULL` and `3i`, loading FFI on demand inside the sandbox (`lj_lex.c:116`, `:127`). By reading, not run: one such value alive at save time cost the machine its state (`persist_typed` in `serializer/eris_lj.c` refuses a cdata value, and `LuaJITArchitecture.java:645-650` then drops the state; a function that merely holds a `1LL` constant persisted, measured), and the prior assessment traced a component call with one as an argument to an `InternalError` (from source on HEAD 62970a0, not run). **Built:** the feature set is `native/luajit/oclj_features.h`: LUA52COMPAT, CHECKHOOK, DISABLE_FFI, and an `#error` for every other option the pinned LuaJIT tests that changes a structure, the bytecode, the unwinder or a gate's instrument. The build copy's `lj_arch.h` includes it (`native/luajit/patch-features-include.sh`), so the archive and every translation unit compiled with `-I<build copy>/src` read the same bytes, and no feature `-D` is left on any command line. `string.format`'s integer conversions stay compiled without FFI (`native/luajit/patch-strfmt-xint-noffi.sh`); without the patch a hot `%x` of an int stitches and runs 1.6-2.1x slower when the strings repeat, 1.2x when each result is new (unpinned, 2026-10-16). **Gates:** `build-native.sh` refuses any `-DLUAJIT_*` in the LuaJIT log but the Makefile's platform ones, checks the preprocessor's verdict through `-I$LJ`, runs an FFI / integer-format gate (`noffi.lua`) and an `nm` check, and in stage 1b compares the archive's GG_State with the probe's (6376 on Windows and on Linux x64, both measured), with a negative arm that must mismatch on every build. `lj52_newstate` refuses a state whose GG_State differs from the shim's or eris_lj's (`negative-control.sh` arms `layout` and `layout-eris`, one per comparison); `penalty_test.c` and the serializer's `test_main.c` make the same check. The blob fingerprint ends in a VM-features word that the build checks against `luajit.exe` (the blob-compatibility row). **Tests:** `security_test.c` FF and BF, harness `d3-sandbox-ffi-literal-refused`, `serializer/tests/fingerprint.lua` and `negctl-fingerprint.sh`. Evidence: `bench/runs/2026-10-16-noffi/` (harness 80/0 additive with the JIT on, 67/0 dropin, 78/0 JIT off, 74/0 sieve, 51/0 stock; the native suites on Windows and Linux; `negative-control.sh` 56/0). **Found 2026-10-16, not fixed:** `%c` of an int past 2^31 differs between compiled and interpreted code (`lj_opt_narrow.c:474-478`); `string.format("%x", math.huge)` (or of `"inf"`, which LuaJIT's `tonumber` accepts and PUC 5.2's does not) returns `8000000000000000` where PUC 5.2 raises; without FFI, `jit.util.tracek` returns an unset value for a KINT64 constant (`lj_ir.c:385-391`; no project code calls it, and the sandbox has no `jit.util`). **Found 2026-10-17, fixed by round 3:** without FFI the bitwise operators were BitOp's 32-bit ones (the 5.2-class row's 2026-10-16 sentence), and they were not even self-consistent: BitOp leaves the conversion of an operand beyond ±2^51 unspecified, and LuaJIT's narrowing exploits that, so `(i * 2^45 - i) | 0` in a loop gave -66 compiled and -33 interpreted at i = 66 (233 of 300 iterations differed), and generated recurrences differed between the JIT and the interpreter in 141 and 125 of 400 (two seeds; round-2 build). Round 3 (the bitwise-operators row below) computes them on int64 and gave 0 of 800 on its first prototype.
- [x] **`string.pack`, `string.unpack` and `string.packsize`: Lua 5.3.6's, in our fork's string library -- decided 2026-10-13, built 2026-10-17.** The first 5.3 functions our fork adds (the 5.2-class row). **Where:** library functions of LuaJIT's `lib_string.c` (LJLIB_CF; `native/luajit/patch-strpack-register.sh` adds three registrations to the build copy, and `native/luajit/oclj_strpack.c` is the code), so `luaopen_string` carries them: jnlua's openlib(STRING) in both natives, luajit.exe, every native test, and the string metatable. A shim wrapper was the alternative; it would have left luajit.exe and every `luaL_openlibs` host without them, so the conformance tests would have run a different path from the one that ships. **What:** Lua 5.3.6's whole format language, with 5.3.6's results and errors -- text, argument number and order -- except the two decided deviations and the VM residuals below: an unpacked integer that needs more than 53 bits is the nearest double of 5.3.6's exact 64-bit value, refused only where 5.3.6 refuses (Plan9k's GPT scan unpacks I8 fields of every drive's sector 1 before it checks the signature; what 5.3.6 itself refuses, an I9..I16 field that does not fit 64 bits, stays refused), with unsigned 8-byte formats (`I8`, `J`, `L`, `T`) wrapping to a negative value first, as 5.3.6's `lua_Integer` does (eight `ff` bytes are -1); and `l`/`L` are 8 bytes everywhere. Integers are int64 in C, never `lua_Integer` (LuaJIT's `luaL_checkinteger` truncates 1.5 to 1, and the shim's `lua_Unsigned` is 32 bits); string arguments convert by 5.3's rules; an `s[n]` length that would wrap 5.3.6's check raises 5.3.6's own "memory allocation error: block too big" without the wrap; a result above 512 bytes is one exact-size block charged to the RAM cap before anything is written, and one of 512 bytes or less is built in a C array, so only its final string is charged (2x the output at the peak, against 3.8-4.6x on LuaJIT's 5.1 `luaL_Buffer`); pack's time is linear in the format, the output and the string arguments it converts, which it reads in both walks (the sandbox-constraints row; [research/wd-blind-spots.md](research/wd-blind-spots.md) 1g). One choice of the port: its converter uses LuaJIT's numeral scanner after its own 5.3 refusals (decided D5), so a numeral with 2^20 or more digits after the radix point (leading zeros counted, trailing zeros not, decimal or hex; `lj_strscan.c:449-453`) or with an exponent of 2^20 or more (`:468`) is refused, where 5.3.6's `strtod` reads the first as its value and the second as inf or 0 (the `RESID-scan` lines of `test/native/pack/deviations.txt`). What the VM decides stays LuaJIT's: NaN payloads are canonicalized; one call returns at most 8000 stack slots; numbers given to `c`/`s`/`z` are formatted by LuaJIT (`string.pack('z', 100000000000000)` gives `1e+14`, 5.3.6 `100000000000000`); a result over the RAM cap is refused as `LUA_ERRMEM` "not enough memory", unpositioned, after every argument check, where 5.3.6 raises a runtime "not enough memory for buffer allocation" (positioned when called non-tail from Lua; `LUA_ERRMEM` if its final string is what fails) and can raise it before it reaches a later bad argument, and D4's text, that 5.3.6 text, is raised here only at `LJ_MAX_STR`, which no OC cap reaches; and function names, positions and method argument numbers in error messages are LuaJIT's, as for every library function (a function called straight from `pcall` is `?`, and a tail-called one loses its name and its position, and its method's argument number shifts). A hot loop of pack calls stitches the trace and does not abort. **Feature tests:** a program that tests for `string.pack` now takes its 5.3 path on our arch. Plan9k's FAT driver does (`lib/msdosfs.lua:23`) and so inherits a bug of Plan9k's own on that path: for an odd cluster `setFATEntry12` packs `bit32.lshift(0xFFFF, 4) + low` as `<I2` (`:158`), which raises `unsigned overflow`, and `makeDirectory` reaches it only after writing the directory entry and zeroing the new cluster (`:1087`-`:1100`), so a FAT12 image (`bin/mount.msdos.lua:22` always mounts FAT12) keeps an entry whose cluster the FAT still marks free. Before this round it took the `bit32` fallback, whose silent truncation gives the intended 16 bits. PUC 5.3.6 raises the same error, so OC's own Lua 5.3 architecture has the bug too; measured by round 2's consumer run (`bench/runs/2026-10-17-strpack/logs/consumers/final.out:40`, against the fallback path at `:57`; PUC 5.3.6 at `ref536-win64.out:40` and `ref536-lp64.out:40`). Not a port defect. **Gates:** `build-native.sh` asserts the registration and the code in the build copy, `FFDEF` x3 in the generated `lj_ffdef.h`, `oclj_strpack.c` warning-free under `-Wall -Wextra`, `OCLJ_LIB_STRPACK 1` through `-I$LJ`, and a luajit.exe gate (`strpack.lua`); the fingerprint word's `pack1` token (the blob-compatibility row). **Tests:** `test/native/run-pack.sh` (308 + 411 conformance cases against checked-in expectations, their provenance against PUC 5.3.6, and `pack_test.c` through the shim: jnlua's opener, Plan9k's GPT header on a text sector, the cap's charge and refusal, the argument checks before that refusal, D4's text at `LJ_MAX_STR`, the C-stack limit), `test/native/run-packfuzz.sh` (differential against PUC 5.3.6; 0 mismatches in 5,000,000 random cases over 50 seeds on 2026-10-17, and 0 in the long-string profile), `test/native/negctl-pack.sh` (28 one-line sabotages and the unregistered library, each caught), `serializer/tests/strpack.lua`, harness `d4-sandbox-string-pack`. Evidence: `bench/runs/2026-10-17-strpack/`.
- [x] **Lua 5.3's bitwise operators with 64-bit results: `&`, `|`, `~`, `<<`, `>>` and unary `~`, in the parser, the interpreter and the JIT -- decided 2026-10-13, semantics decided 2026-10-17, built 2026-10-17.** Without FFI (the FFI row above) the operators computed LuaJIT's 32-bit BitOp results (`1 << 32` was 1, `1.5 | 0` was 2, `"3" & 1` raised the arithmetic error), and the JIT and the interpreter disagreed beyond ±2^51 (the FFI row's 2026-10-17 finding). **What:** Lua 5.3.6's semantics on a VM whose numbers are all doubles. An operand converts as 5.3.6's `luaV_tointeger` in mode 0: an integral double in [-2^63, 2^63) (2^63 itself is refused, as `string.pack` refuses it), or a numeric string by 5.3's reading (`"0x10" | 0` is 16; a hex string wraps modulo 2^64), through `native/luajit/oclj_num53.h`, which `oclj_strpack.c` now shares; `&`, `|` and `~` wrap on int64; `<<` and `>>` are 5.3.6's `luaV_shiftl` (`>>` logical, a count of 64 or more gives 0, a negative count shifts the other way). Errors are 5.3.6's, `number has no integer representation` and `attempt to perform bitwise operation on`, with 5.3.6's choice of operand to blame, LuaJIT's word order where a variable is named (`on local 't' (a table value)`), and LuaJIT's type name, so a metatable's string `__name` is not used where 5.3.6, and our `string.pack`, use it (`a table value` for 5.3.6's `a Foo value`; LuaJIT's arithmetic errors do the same; U5-A, measured 2026-10-17). **The decided deviation (U1 = B, 2026-10-17):** a 64-bit result is returned as a double only when that double converts back to it, i.e. when it has at most 53 significant bits; any other result raises `result of '<op>' has more than 53 significant bits (mask each operand to 32 bits first)`, so every result that does not raise is exactly 5.3.6's. The user's reasons: a wrong value is always worse than an error, and the error has a remedy in user code, masking before shifting (`(n & 0xFFFFFFFF) >> 8`), which the message names (the hint was added after the build, below). The alternative, the nearest double (round 2's choice for `string.unpack`), was prototyped first: OpenOS's 5.3 `bit32` polyfill then differed from PUC 5.3.6 in 357 of 3298 calls, 331 of them silently (`bit32.rshift(-1, 2)` gave 0 where 5.3.6 gives 4294967295). What raises: `>>` of most negative numbers (`-1 >> 1`; `(n >> 8) & 0xFF` for n = -2, at the `>>`), 5.3's usual 32-bit rotate `((x >> n) | (x << (32 - n))) & 0xFFFFFFFF` for n from 1 to 10 (below), 5.3's `math.maxinteger` idiom `~0 >> 1`, `(1 << 62) | 1`, `~(2^53)`, `2^60 | 1`; what computes, equal to 5.3.6: `1 << 62`, `~0 >> 11` (2^53 - 1), `~(2^53 - 1)`, `-2^63 | 0`, the masked `((n & 0xFFFFFFFF) >> 8) & 0xFF` (round 3's prototype against PUC 5.3.6). The parser folds a constant expression only when its operands convert and its result is exact; otherwise the run time raises, as 5.3.6 does for `1.5 & 1`. LuaJIT's `~>>` (an arithmetic right shift, not 5.3) is 64-bit under the same rules, a count of 64 or more filling with the sign, and the compound assignments follow (`x <<= 40` gives 2^40). **The literal residual (U9):** LuaJIT's lexer reads a numeric literal as the nearest double before any operator sees it, so ANY hex literal with more than 53 significant bits is rounded silently, not only those of 2^63 and above: `0x00ff00ff00ff00ff & 0xff` is 0 here and 255 in 5.3.6, and `0xffffffffffffffff` (2^64), `0x7fffffffffffffff` and `0x8000000000000000` (both 2^63) raise as operands. No census tree has such a literal (grep, 2026-10-17). **Where:** six LuaJIT patches in `patch-strpack-register.sh`'s form (`native/luajit/patch-bitop-{parse,vm,meta,record,fold,bcdump}.sh`), a seventh for LuaJIT's own tools (`patch-bitop-jitlib.sh`, below), and five tracked files the build copies in: `oclj_bitop.h`, the rules, shared by the parser's folding, the C path and the recorder so they cannot disagree; `oclj_bitop_meta.c`, the interpreter's slow path and the errors, included by `lj_meta.c`; `oclj_bitop_rec.c`, the recorder; `oclj_bitop_fold.c`, the width bound, included by `lj_opt_fold.c`, whose `LJFOLD` rule body stays in `lj_opt_fold.c`, the only file `buildvm -m folddef` scans; and `oclj_num53.h`. The interpreter's fast path (`vm_x64.dasc`) computes in 64 bits, converts the result to a double and back and compares, and an inexact result goes to the C path, which recomputes from the untouched operands and raises; a string or a non-integral operand takes the C path too. **The JIT:** the seven bytecodes (`BC_BNOT` to `BC_BSAR`) record as int64 IR. A number operand is converted with a guard; the result is a `CONV num.i64` marked exact (`OCLJ_IRCONV_EXACT`, bit 0x400 of the CONV's op2, which LuaJIT 2.1 does not use, inside `IRCONV_MODEMASK`, so `cse_conv` never merges a marked with an unmarked conversion, and outside the fold key, so every CONV rule still matches), followed at once by its guard, a conversion back and `EQ.i64`. A result that is inexact at record time aborts the trace (the interpreter is about to raise). `lj_opt_fold.c` gets four changes: the seven int64 bit folds that LuaJIT compiles only with FFI are compiled always (without FFI, `BAND/BOR/BXOR KINT64 KINT64` folded silently to the left operand); `narrow_convert` never narrows an int64 CONV; the exact-mark rule, `(i64)(num)y ==> y` when the inner conversion is marked and the outer is not, so a result that a later operator uses never makes a round trip through a double and a value carried between iterations becomes an int64 PHI; and the width rule, the same fold when y provably lies in [-2^53, 2^53), which is what removes the guard of a result that fits. The width rule's soundness rests on the review's argument (types are iteration-invariant, `lj_opt_loop.c:343-352`; every value from outside the trace enters either as a double, through a conversion the bound scores 64, or as an int-typed value, a narrowed slot such as the loop index or an int from a parent snapshot, which scores 33 from its type alone), not on the round-3 design's step that a loop copy's bound is at most the pre-roll's. Both rules cross a PHI only to an int64-typed y (the review's fix1: without it, `x = i | 0` handed the int loop index to an int64 PHI, `loop_unroll` raised a type instability, and the loop was blacklisted). **Speed** (the prototype, best of 3, same run, no Java running; today's build, then round 3): bit loop 0.35 and 0.35-0.40 ns per iteration; RGB pack/unpack 3.6-3.7 and 3.6-3.8 ns; CRC32 by table 2.8-2.9 and 7.2-7.3 ns per byte; CRC32 bit-serial 48-49 and 70 ns per byte (the CRC32 row below). Shipped build, the same alternating run (`bench/runs/2026-10-17-bitop/logs/bench/`): bit loop 0.30-0.35 and 0.30-0.40 ns per iteration; RGB 3.7 and 3.7 ns; CRC32 by table 2.8-2.9 and 7.2 ns per byte; CRC32 bit-serial 48-49 and 69.5-70 ns per byte, the prototype's. **Persistence:** folded constants change meaning, so `BCDUMP_VERSION` is 0x80 and the blob fingerprint's word gains `bit64` (the blob-compatibility row); `serializer/tests/fixtures/forin-identity.blob` was regenerated (on the prototype only its three dumps' version bytes differed; shipped: the same, 8 bytes in all, the three version bytes 0x02 to 0x80 and five inside the frame-link payload the check masks). **Gates:** `build-native.sh` patches and asserts the six files in the build copy, and `lib_jit.c` for the tools, and copies and `cmp`s the five tracked ones; `host/buildvm_arch.h` is regenerated from the patched `vm_x64.dasc`; `OCLJ_OPS_BIT64 1` and `BCDUMP_VERSION 0x80` through `-I$LJ`; the new files warning-free under `-Wall -Wextra`; a luajit.exe gate; stage 4's `bit64` token. GG_State: 6376 on the prototype, unchanged (no new bytecode, IR op or metamethod); shipped: 6376. **Tests:** `test/native/run-bitop.sh` (7467 runtime cases, 63 literal folds and 437 `~>>` cases against checked-in expectations, JIT on and off; their provenance against PUC 5.3.6 with the decided rule applied per operator, differing in exactly `test/native/bitop/deviations.txt`'s ids, and a raw PUC run showing that the rule raises exactly where 5.3.6's integer has more than 53 significant bits; the operators' and `string.pack`'s readers agreeing; `bitop_test.c` through the shim), `test/native/run-bitfuzz.sh` (differential against PUC 5.3.6, with `E:INEXACT` as its own error kind; a campaign of 50 seeds x 20 000 cases x 16 tuples, 16 000 000 compared, 0 mismatches in every mode), `test/native/run-bitjit.sh` (the review's generator of compiled loops, five JIT option sets and fresh and shared closures against `-joff` and the PUC twin, 0 differences required: on the prototype, 20 seeds x 2000 loops x 7 starts gave 0; 115,529 of 280,000 shared runs raised, on seeds 1-4 79% of them with the new 53-bit error and the rest with `number has no integer representation`, and 28-32% of the raises came in the same iteration as a compiled trace's exit (the review's `texit` hook on seeds 1-4, on the implemented build), so the error exits run; the recurrence and adversarial loops; and fix1's check, the eight p4 loops, seven identity bit operations on the loop index such as `x = i | 0` and a mask control, compiling with no type-instability abort and none blacklisted), `test/native/negctl-bitop.sh` (27 one-line sabotages, each caught by the suite named for it), `security_test.c` FF12, `serializer/tests/bitop.lua` (a coroutine frozen inside a compiled 64-bit loop persists and resumes), harness `d5-sandbox-bitwise`. **Found by the round's first harness runs, fixed in this round (2026-10-17):** (1) **5.3's usual 32-bit rotate raises.** `((x >> n) | (x << (32 - n))) & 0xFFFFFFFF` joins its two pieces with `|` into up to 64 - n significant bits before the mask drops the high ones, so a right rotation by 1 to 10 raises (by 2, 6 and 7, SHA-256's counts, for 82-99% of random 32-bit words; by 10, for 25%), and so does the left form `((x << n) | (x >> (32 - n))) & 0xFFFFFFFF` by 22 to 31; right by 11 to 31 and left by 1 to 21 never raise (10004 words x every n, JIT on and off alike). PUC 5.3.6 computes all of them, and the round-2 build computed them modulo 2^32. It stopped the harness's own SHA-256 benchmark through `bench/oc/compat.lua`'s rotate (`p1-sha256` failed with `[string "compat-bitops"]:11: result of '|' has more than 53 significant bits`, the message before the hint, in all five round-3 arms that took the operator path; the stock arm takes `bit32` and passed), and with it `bench/oc/run-standalone.sh`'s sha256 row and its `contract.lua` and `compat-branches.lua` gates. The remedy is one mask, on the shifted piece: both `bench/compat.lua` and `bench/oc/compat.lua` now rotate as `(a >> n) | ((a << (32 - n)) & 0xFFFFFFFF)`, which gives the published digest on round 3, JIT on and off, and the same digest as before on PUC 5.3.6 (Windows and Linux), PUC 5.4.8 and the round-2 build; over 10004 words x n = 0..31 the two forms return the same value in every call where the old one does not raise, on all of those VMs. The README's dialect section gives it as the decided rule's main cost, with the remedy. (2) **The hint:** the user kept the decided rule and gave the message its remedy, `result of '<op>' has more than 53 significant bits (mask each operand to 32 bits first)`, for every operator. (3) **LuaJIT's own tools:** `jit.util.funcbc` and `jit.util.tracesnap` returned bit-field words as signed int32, and LuaJIT's `jit/bc.lua` and `jit/dump.lua` decode them with the native operators, so under 64-bit operators `(ins >> 8) & 0xff` of a word with bit 31 set (every forward jump and FORI) has 56 significant bits and raised: `luajit -bl` stopped at the first such word, and `-jdump`, whose default modes include bytecode, aborted every trace it recorded through one (round 3's review). `native/luajit/patch-bitop-jitlib.sh` patches `lib_jit.c` so both return the words unsigned; the tools then decode exactly what upstream's 32-bit operators did, and `-jdump` and `-bl` work again. It changes no operator, and the sandbox has no `jit.*` modules. Evidence: `bench/runs/2026-10-17-bitop/`. **Not in this round:** the bitwise metamethods and `//`, the CRC32 speed, and the smaller follow-ups (the rows below).
- [ ] **CRC32 under the 64-bit operators: a range guard in the integer domain and the table-index fold -- added 2026-10-17; the table-index fold shipped 2026-10-20, and the row stays open.** Round 3 (the row above) costs table-driven CRC32 7.2-7.3 ns per byte against today's 2.8-2.9, and bit-serial CRC32 70 against 48-49 (its prototype, best of 3; PUC 5.3.6 took 32.4 and 121 in an earlier session of the same round). The cause, read from the IR: `crc >> 8` unmasked has a 57-bit bound, so its result keeps its guard, and every value loaded from the table takes a checked conversion. Two changes were prototyped by round 3's review and are not shipped: a speculative range guard in the integer domain, off the dependency chain and in the loop body only (`(y ~>> 53) + 1 <= 1` unsigned, with the arithmetic shift the prototype emits), in place of the round trip through a double; and a fold of `CONV int.num (CONV num.i64 y)` for a small y, for table indices. What remained (as written 2026-10-17): both under B's semantics, with the generator and `negctl-bitop.sh` arms for each, measured against today's 2.9 ns per byte. **Status 2026-10-20: the table-index fold shipped; the row stays open.** **What shipped** [S]: `native/luajit/patch-bitop-fold.sh`'s hunk 11, in `simplify_conv_int_num`: `CONV int.num (CONV num.i64 y)` becomes the unguarded `CONV int.i64 y` when `oclj_bitwidth` bounds y at 32 bits or fewer, so that y is exactly an int32 and the removed check could never fail, and never across a PHI (the user's policy, decided 2026-10-20). It speculates nothing, and it reaches every consumer that narrows a bit result to an int: a table key, a library integer argument, a variable shift count. It ships with `run-bitjit.sh`'s new idx part (`t_idx.lua`, `t_cnt33.lua` and `t_idx2.lua` against `-joff` and through `twin.lua` against PUC 5.3.6, and `phiidx.lua`, which reads the compiled loops), `t_rg2.lua` in the floor, the generator's `idx` and `rg2` modes, and three `negctl-bitop.sh` arms, WIDTHIDX33, PHIIDX and NOLOOPFOLD (30 arms in all). A missing PUC now makes `run-bitjit.sh` say PROVED NOTHING unless `OCLJ_LUA53=none`. **Measured** [M] (the main session's timing chain, nine interleaved rounds, 2026-10-20, CPU load 7-9%, no Java running, every result equal across the four builds; and its b0 companion, five rounds; medians in ns per byte, on these benches on this machine): table-driven CRC32 (`bench_bit2.lua`'s crc-table, this row's number) 7.20 before and 3.70 with the fold, 0.51 of before in the same round [0.50-0.51], against round 2's 2.90 (the fold 1.28 of it [1.24-1.28]) and PUC 5.3.6's 32.4; bit-serial CRC32 69.5-70 before and 70.0 with the fold, against round 2's 48.5 and PUC's 120.5. Elsewhere the fold read 0.66 on a mixed loop, 0.50 on flag words shifted by a masked count, 0.61-0.63 on identity operations that index a table with a masked key, 0.98 on phi2, 0.51-0.52 on the other table-driven CRC32 loops (`bench_crcx.lua`'s crcprobe and crcmb, `bench_idx.lua`'s crct and crctm), and 1.00-1.02 on every other row. **Not shipped: rg2**, the speculative range guard, prototyped, verified and measured beside the fold. On top of the fold it bought 0-5% on the CRC32 rows and 21% on the mixed loop; it cost 3-7x on loops whose exact results leave [-2^53, 2^53), all legal under the decided rule (hi60 6.64x, sh40 7.31x, top8 5.0x, half 3.64x and flag63 3.17x of before, 6.27x of the fold alone), and 2x on identity operations of doubles past 2^53; with `-Omaxside=0`, hi60 took 11.5 ns against 0.5. Its boundary tests stay (`t_rg2.lua`, the generator's `rg2` mode). **What remains:** bit-serial CRC32, 70 ns per byte against round 2's 48.5, and table-driven CRC32's last quarter, 3.6-3.7 against 2.9. **Next step for this row:** a design pass on loop-carried widths, an inductive bound for a PHI such as the bit-serial loop's `b`, which the width rule scores 64 (an idea, not measured). Beside it, FIX-NUMCONV (item (5) of the round-3 follow-ups row below) shipped on 2026-10-21: it removes identity operations' result guards and leaves CRC32's, whose values come from table loads (crc-table, crct and crctm read 1.00 of before in its timing chain) [M]. **Verified** [M]: the final check of the working tree, 57 checks passed and none failed (both builds, every bit-operator suite on Windows and AlmaLinux 8, the full 30-arm `negctl-bitop.sh` sweep, the other native runners, timing that repeats the numbers above); the release natives rebuilt into `build/native` equal its builds object for object, and the JVM harness's seven arms on Windows and on AlmaLinux 8 read as in the glibc round. Evidence: `bench/runs/2026-10-20-crc32/` (`logs/design-chain/`, `logs/design-b0/`, `logs/final-main/`, `logs/release/`, `scripts/`, `design/`); the JIT guide's 8.8.
- [ ] **Bitwise metamethods, `//` and `__idiv` -- after the CRC32 row, added 2026-10-17.** Lua 5.3's `__band`, `__bor`, `__bxor`, `__shl`, `__shr`, `__bnot` and `__idiv`, and the floor-division operator `//`, which LuaJIT does not parse; upstream a2ce8114 backported neither (LuaJIT `doc/extensions.html:432-436`). One change, because both add metamethod slots: each MMDEF slot grows `gcroot[]` and moves GG_State, so the layout gates and every 6376 in docs and tests move once, together. The metamethods also need a vmeta continuation path like `vmeta_arith`, recorder support and eris continuation frames (`bcmode_mm(BC_BAND..)` is none today, and `eris_lj.c` refuses a mismatched site); `//` needs a token, its bytecode or a lowering, and a decision on its semantics without an integer subtype. Why it can come later: round 3's C path raises an error wherever 5.3 would look for a metamethod (the type error, or `number has no integer representation` for a number or numeric string with no integer value, where 5.3.6 first tries `__band` and the rest through a number's or the string metatable), so no working program depends on one being ignored, and the census trees use none of the six bitwise names (AxisOS, MineOS, QuickOS, OpenOS, Plan9k and OC's kernel, grep 2026-10-17). `//` is what still keeps MineOS's `Color.lua` 5.3 path from compiling (the MineOS row).
- [ ] **Round 3's smaller follow-ups, none blocking -- added 2026-10-17.** (1) **Narrower operand widths, speed only:** `oclj_bitwidth` (`native/luajit/oclj_bitop_fold.c`) scores an int operand 33 bits whatever its source; one known to be a u8 or a u16 (9 or 17 bits) would let more results drop their guard. (2) **The scanner residual, for the operators too:** a numeric string with an exponent of 2^20 or more is not a number to LuaJIT's scanner (`lj_strscan.c:468`), so `"1e-1048576" | 0` raises the type error where 5.3.6 gives 0, and `"1e1048576" | 0` the type error where 5.3.6 says `number has no integer representation` (round 3's prototype, 2026-10-17). It is `string.pack`'s `RESID-scan` (the string.pack row), through the same `oclj_num53.h`; a `strtod` fallback there would fix both. (3) **A loop-invariant shift count outside 0..63 aborts the trace:** the recorder guards a variable count `ULT 64` and aborts outside it, so such a loop runs interpreted; speed only. (4) **Instrument:** the Windows PUC 5.3.6 build (`bench/vendor/lua-5.3.6/src/lua.exe`) mis-rounds long hex-float strings: `"0x1.0000000000000801p52" | 0`, and `tonumber` of it, give 4503599627370496 there, where the Linux PUC 5.3.6 and round 3's prototype give the correctly rounded 4503599627370497 (2026-10-17). An expectation that depends on such a string must come from a Linux PUC. (5) ~~**FIX-NUMCONV, a round of its own, after the select fix -- added 2026-10-20, speed only.**~~ **Done 2026-10-21: FIX-NUMCONV shipped** (JIT guide 3.10, 3.11, 11.5c, 11.7, P60-P62; the round's archive `bench/runs/2026-10-21-numconv/`: `logs/final-main/`, `logs/release/`, `logs/step1-timing/`, `scripts/`, `design/`, `upstream/` (the verification of #1535's fix, the syntax probe and the pin move's census); its scratch `claude-tmp/oclj/numconv/`). **What it was** (2026-10-20): an operand's own int64, `CONV i64.num` of a double, is always exactly a double (`cvttsd2si` gives the double's integral part, or INT64_MIN), so the result guard of an identity operation that folds to it (`x | 0`, `x & -1`, `x ~ 0`, `x << 0`, `x >> 0`) can never fail; it stayed, because the width rule bounds ranges and scores such a value 64. The CRC32 follow-up's verifier (verify-rg2) proposed a one-line fold for it; in its smoke run, on the rg2 prototype with the fold added, `ident.lua`'s identity rows read 1.4-1.6 ns against round 3's build's 1.6-2.0, which has no table-index fold; with the table-index fold alone those rows read 1.10-1.20 (the CRC32 follow-up's timing chain). Beside it, measured on PUC 5.3.6 and not prototyped: `x << k`, and x shifted right arithmetically by k, have at most 53 significant bits whenever x has (all 25,600,000 pairs tried, k in 0..63, verify-rg2's `shiftexact.lua`, re-run 2026-10-20), so the result guard of a shift of an exact operand should never fail either (measured on PUC 5.3.6, not proved; JIT guide 3.11). **What changed** [S]: `native/luajit/patch-bitop-fold.sh`'s hunk 7 gains a rule in `simplify_conv_i64_num`, on a line of its own after the width rule, inside fix1's block and before `PHIBARRIER` (the user's decision; verify-rg2's form merged into the width rule's line, which breaks `verify_content`'s count of `<= 54)` and negctl's WIDTH55 anchor): `if (oclj_isnumconv(J, fleft->op1)) return fleft->op1;` (build copy, `lj_opt_fold.c` :1196). The predicate is `static int oclj_isnumconv(jit_State *J, IRRef ref)` in `native/luajit/oclj_bitop_fold.c` (:170-175, its argument in the comment above it): y is an `IR_CONV`, typed `i64`, with no guard, from `IRT_NUM`. `native/build-native.sh`'s `bitop_wgate.c` calls it (:1668), so the `-Wall -Wextra` gate compiles it. NADD 63 to 70, NDEL 8; `verify_content` also requires `oclj_isnumconv(` on exactly one line, the call on `fleft->op1`, and that call inside the rules' block, below the width rule's call; both checks beyond the prototype's, the call inside the rules' block (the code implementer's) and each rule's call followed directly by its own return (the fixer's), are kept by the main session's decision of 2026-10-22, and nothing about them is open. Of 80 objects only `lj_opt_fold.o` changes, on Windows (`f6d916e2`; `build/native`'s `1d018acc`) and in AlmaLinux 8 with `OCLJ_GLIBC_MAX=2.28` (`57970a51`; `33a2cc50`) [M]. **Why it is sound** [S][M]: x64 compiles such a y to one `cvttsd2si r64`, whose value, the double's integral part in [-2^63, 2^63) or else -2^63, is always exactly a double, so the result guard's round trip gives y back in every rounding mode (a probe of the guard's instructions on this machine's CPU: 0 mismatches in 21,179,656 doubles under each of eight MXCSR settings), and the constant fold computes the same instruction (`lj_vm_num2i64`). It reads no guard and no mark, only y's opcode, type and mode, so it holds in every iteration and across a PHI, by an induction that rests on the guards emitted before y [S][I], and it returns an existing ref of the folded type. It is a fact of exactness, not of range, and must never enter `oclj_bitwidth`, where it would compose (`x | 1` for x = 2^53 would lose its guard). x64 only (the Parked platforms row). - [ ] **An upstream collision: `PROTO_BITOP` lies inside the closure counter -- found 2026-10-17, to report upstream.** `PROTO_BITOP` is 0x80 (`lj_obj.h:404`), inside the 3-bit closure counter at 0x20..0xe0 (`:409-411`, `lj_func.c:134-135`), so `flags >= PROTO_CLC_POLY` (`lj_record.c:802`, `:1795`) is always true in a function that uses a bitwise operator: its immutable upvalues are never constified, and its calls specialise to the prototype. Measured in round 3's design work: one `UREFC` with a bit operator, a constant without it. Speed only, and the flags byte has no free bit, so the fix is not a one-liner; not fixed in our fork. Report it with the two drafted upstream reports (the upstream-reports row under v2).
- [x] **Compiled `select(k, ...)` with a large negative `k` returned nil where the interpreter raises -- found 2026-10-20, fixed in our fork the same day (JIT guide P54 and 11.5b), and upstream on 2026-10-21 (`a4c0645`).** The divergence is upstream LuaJIT's (the 2026-09-01 upstream binary behaves alike [M]), and by reading upstream's v2.1 tip still had it on 2026-10-20 (`dd3b243`): `rec_varg` and lj_opt_narrow.c were unchanged there [M: compared in the round's bare clone]; no tip build was run. Reported upstream as LuaJIT issue #1535, which upstream fixed in `a4c0645` and closed (**Upstream**, below). Evidence: `bench/runs/2026-10-21-select/` (`logs/final-main/`, `logs/release/`, `scripts/`, `design/`); the round's scratch is `claude-tmp/oclj/sel/` (`impl/`, the implementer's; `sweep/`, a read-only sweep; `docs/`, this update's). **Found** [M] by the CRC32 follow-up's verifier (verify-folda; `probes/selbug.lua` in its scratch, `claude-tmp/oclj/crc/verify-folda/`). The input: a vararg function called with 7 varargs runs `local v = select(K[i], ...)` in a loop over i = 1..200, with `K[i] = 100`, beyond the varargs, for all but three i, so the loop compiles with k = 100; then `K[150] = -2147483628`. Under `-joff` that call raises "index out of range"; compiled, it returned nil (the probe prints `n 30 E 200`, where `-joff` prints `E 30 E 200`). The shipped build and the 2026-09-01 upstream binary behaved alike, and no bit operator is involved. **The mechanism** [S: `rec_varg`, lj_record.c ~:1989-2089 at the pin, read by the main session, the implementer and the sweep; M: `test/native/bitop/t_select.lua` on the shipped build, scratch `sel/impl/runs/diag-base-2/`]. In a vararg function's own frame, `rec_varg` records `y = select(k, ...)` on one of three paths, chosen by the k it records (a recorded k of 0 or below aborts the recording): (A) a constant k, a guard of the frame-size word `fr` against the constant `frofs + 8*k`; (B) a k within the varargs, their count from `fr`, an unsigned bounds check on k-1, then the load; (C) a k beyond them, a guard `fr < 8*k + frofs`, no load, and nil. Path C computes `8*k + frofs` in 32 bits. Three classes of runtime k passed that guard where the interpreter raises, and each gave nil: (1) a k whose 8k wraps past the frame: selbug's -2^31 + 20, -2^29 + 20 and their kin; (2) with no wrap at all, a k from m+1 to 0 in a call that passed m < 0 varargs, that is fewer arguments than the function's fixed parameters, where `fr` is below `frofs` (k = 0 and k = -1); (3) a k that narrowing wrapped: in `select(x + c, ...)`, with x a double and c a constant of 16 bits, `narrow_conv_emit` leaves the outermost ADD or SUB of an index conversion unchecked (lj_opt_narrow.c ~:386-392; upstream's design, which hands that check to the consumer's unsigned bounds check), so an x + c outside int32 reached path C within 2^15 of -2^31 (it passed where 8k then wrapped past the frame) or of 2^31 (it passed in a call with 2 fewer arguments than parameters). The interpreter (lib_base.c `select`) raises for k = 0, for a k below minus the vararg count, and for every number outside int32, which `lj_lib_checkint` converts with a C cast that gives -2^31 on this build [S][M: `sel/impl/probes/interp1-base-joff.txt`]. The verifier's first reading named class 1 only. **The fix** [S]: `native/luajit/patch-bitop-record.sh`, hunks 4 and 5, in the script that already patched `lj_record.c`, the only one that does (JIT guide 1.2 and 8.2 step 0). `OCLJ_SELECT_KMAX`, 2^27, is defined above `rec_varg`; the recorder aborts when the k it records is above it, as it already did for k < 0; and on path C a guard `ULT k-1, 2^27` comes before the shift, so the trace exits unless 1 <= k <= 2^27 and the interpreter computes select, raising or returning. After that guard `8*k + frofs` is at most 2^30 + 1619 for any function the parser makes (`numparams` <= `LJ_MAX_LOCVAR`, 200; the code comment's looser bound, `LJ_MAX_SLOTS`, gives 2^30 + 2019) and cannot wrap, and no k within the varargs is refused: a frame holds at most LUAI_MAXSTACK (65500) of them. Only `lj_record.o` changes, of 80 objects, on Windows and in AlmaLinux 8 [M]. A guard of k >= 1 alone, built as a variant, still differed from `-joff` in 11 of 15 option sets, through class 3's k wrapped to 2^31 - 1 [M: `sel/impl/runs/bitjit-sel-gt0-2`]. **The constant path's C overflow** [S][M]: path A's record-time `frofs + 8*(int32_t)k` is int arithmetic in C, so a constant k from about 2^28 (from 2^28 - 2 with no fixed parameters, from 2^28 - 202 with the most) overflows, which C leaves undefined, and our build compiles `lj_record.c` without `-fwrapv` [M: the compile lines in the round's build logs]. The shipped build wrapped it: a constant `select(2^28, ...)` in `f(n, ...)` compiled the guard `LT fr, -2147483621`, which always fails, so the loop exited every iteration and grew a chain of side traces (25 trace starts in its 300 iterations; the probe's second loop, `select(2^29 + 5, ...)`, also wrapped, into a guard `LT fr, +67` that passes with the right nil, and took one), with the interpreter's values [M: `sel/impl/probes/konst1-base.dump`, `konst1-base.jv`]. With the fix the recording aborts before that expression is computed (`NYI: bytecode VARG`), with the same values (`konst1-b2.jv`). **k from 2^28 up** [S][I]: for an exact int32 k >= 1, upstream's path-C guard passed only for a k beyond the varargs, so its nil was right, and the 32-bit wrap only turned some passes into exits; the positive k that passed wrongly were narrowing-wrapped ones (class 3). With the fix such loops run in the interpreter, with the same values. **Tests** [S][M]: `test/native/bitop/t_select.lua`, 110 cases: four call shapes, each of `rec_varg`'s paths and `'#'`, k from a double, a string, narrowing's ADD and SUB, a constant beyond int16, a 32-bit bit result through the table-index fold's `CONV int.i64` and the loop index, side traces, and 27 constants; each case feeds k in range, past the end, negative, 0, wrapping, large, fractional and outside int32. `run-bitjit.sh`'s new part 5, `sel`, in its default parts, compares it with `-joff` under 15 option sets (`SELOPTS`: the idx part's nine and six for exits, side traces, ABC and narrowing) and its `puc` part (106 cases, integral k below 2^31, where select means the same) with PUC 5.3.6. It requires each path's guard, the fix's guard and three loops recorded with k = 2^27 to compile, and `t_select-kmax` fails if a loop recorded only with a k above 2^27 compiles a select trace. **Arms** [M]: `negctl-bitop.sh`'s SELGUARD (the guard removed) is caught by `sel:t_select`, 11-53 cases in each of the 15 sets; SELRECK (the record-time bound removed), which changes no value, by `sel:kmax`, 2-3 of 3 loops in each set; four runs with `OCLJ_NEGCTL_ARMS=SELGUARD,SELRECK`, canon and restore passing in each (`sel/impl/runs/s-b2-negctl-1` to `-4`). 32 arms in all. **Checked** [M] (the implementer's): `run-bitjit.sh`, all five parts with PUC, passes on Windows (63 s; `sel/impl/runs/s-b2-bitjit/`) and in AlmaLinux 8 with `OCLJ_GLIBC_MAX=2.28` and the Linux PUC (`sel/impl/alma1/`), t_select with 0 differences in all 15 sets; run-bitop, run-bitfuzz and nine other native runners pass (`runs/s-b2-suites/`); and selbug prints `-joff`'s line with the JIT on (`runs/selbug-1/`). **Verified** [M] by the main session on the working tree (2026-10-20 22:08-22:31Z; the archive's `logs/final-main/`): the round's final check (`scripts/final-check.sh`, md5 `d45910e9`) passed 69 checks, 0 failing, in 1397 s: fresh builds on Windows and in AlmaLinux 8 with `OCLJ_GLIBC_MAX=2.28` (only `lj_record.o` differs of 80 objects, the implementer's on each platform; GLIBC_2.17 at most); `run-bitjit.sh`'s five parts with PUC on both platforms, t_select at 0 in all 15 sets; the fail-firsts on `build/native`'s natives (the sel part fails in all 15 sets, 12-53 cases a set on Windows and 15-52 in AlmaLinux 8, and `t_select-kmax` in 2-3 loops a set; Real OC does the sort but not the sweep, so `for _,v in ipairs(t)` with a yield in the body fails to save on stock — measured on a live machine: `perms[ipairs aux] = nil` while `perms[ipairs]` is present, and the aux is a stable per-call object that simply is not permed. Two objects on our build. **Status 2026-10-03:** The flattener half was superseded by inheritance. No Java flattener was written: OC's own `PersistenceAPI` runs as the last API of the inherited `initialize()` (LuaJITArchitecture.java:142-144). OC's "sort" is also a no-op: PersistenceAPI.scala:68 discards the result of `sortWith`, and :69 walks the keys in `lua_next` order, which is harmless only while no value reachable from `_G` has two names. The sweep is open. Nothing in src/, native/ or the kernel adds builtin upvalues to perms, and the serializer refuses the ipairs aux as a C function by value (serializer/eris_lj.c:1697). The sweep exists only in the test rigs (serializer/tests/forin.lua:58-73, m2.lua:42-60, m3.lua:53-72, contract.lua:45-60). **Status 2026-10-10:** retitled for what is left, since the flattener half is OC's own (above). The sweep has one object to add, `ipairs_aux`, not two: `pairs`' upvalue is `_G.next`, already named (lib_base.c:107-124); the build's luajit.exe with OC's library set finds no other (2026-10-10), and the shim's own globals are C functions without upvalues (lj52shim.c:2141-2157, :2731; eris_lj.c:3213). Also tracked here, moved from the sandbox-constraints row: the short-string `gmatch` iterator, a runtime C closure the serializer refuses (serializer/eris_lj.c:1697), which the sandbox gets whenever `#s < SHORT_STRING` (build/native/kernel/machine.lua:138, :599-600). The fix proposed there: the pure-Lua `gmatch` at every length, as a kernel site asserted in build-kernel.sh, or `SHORT_STRING = 0`, which would also move `find`, `match` and `gsub` to pure Lua (:544, :682). One milestone shape covers both, not yet written: save while yielded inside the loop body, restore, compare, seen failing on HEAD first. Stock fails the `ipairs` shape too ([research/os-shape-census.md](research/os-shape-census.md):74); stock's `gmatch` is unchecked.

  - **Found** [M]: the .so of rounds 1 to 3, linked in Ubuntu 26.04 under WSL (glibc 2.43, gcc 15.2), needed GLIBC_2.38 (`fmod`, `__isoc23_strtoul`), 2.35 (`_dl_find_object`), 2.34 (`pthread_*`), 2.32 (`pthread_sigmask`) and 2.29 (`exp`, `log`, `log2`, `pow`). In AlmaLinux 8 (glibc 2.28), `ldd -r` answers ``version `GLIBC_2.38' not found`` for it. So it could have loaded on neither Ubuntu 22.04, Debian 12 nor RHEL 9 [I: their glibc versions], and every check on the build host passed it. A trial of HEAD 3adfeaf in AlmaLinux 8 (gcc 8.5.0, binutils 2.30) built both variants needing GLIBC_2.17 at most. But six watchdog imports (`pthread_create`, `pthread_join`, `pthread_condattr_setclock`, `pthread_setname_np`, `pthread_sigmask`, `pthread_attr_setstacksize`) were unversioned, and `libpthread.so.0` was not NEEDED, because the Linux `LINK_EXTRA` was `-static-libgcc` alone. Inside a JVM they bound to the libpthread the JVM had loaded (the row above); outside one, `ldd -r` reports `undefined symbol: pthread_create` [M]. The native suites shim, security, mem and wd did not link there either: `dlopen`, `dlsym`, `dlclose` and `dlerror` were undefined, because `luaL_openlibs` pulls LuaJIT's `lib_package`, and before glibc 2.34 those functions live in libdl [M]. Ubuntu could show none of this. There every flag combination links (27 of 27), because glibc 2.34 folded libpthread and libdl into libc, and Ubuntu's `libpthread.a` and `libdl.a` are 8-byte empty archives [M].
  - **The floor, the user's decision (2026-10-19):** GLIBC_2.28, the glibc of AlmaLinux/RHEL 8, the oldest we can build on, load and harness-test. A release .so needs no symbol version newer than GLIBC_2.28, and gradle refuses one that does. That is the supported floor: x86_64 Linux with glibc 2.28 or newer, RHEL, AlmaLinux and Rocky 8+, Debian 10+ and Ubuntu 20.04+ [I: their glibc versions; tested on AlmaLinux 8.10 and Ubuntu 26.04.1, the row above]. It is a policy, not where loading stops: today's release .so needs at most GLIBC_2.17 [M: `bench/runs/2026-10-19-glibc/logs/build-alma/glibc.txt`], so it would most likely load on glibc 2.17 to 2.27 too [I], but such systems are untested and unsupported. Below the .so's highest need, `System.load` fails, OC's factory reports no native (in ocelot-brain's port, `LuaStateFactory.scala:318-339`), and the mod registers no LuaJIT architecture (`OCLuaJIT.java:59-66`) [S; I for GTNH OpenComputers' own factory; not run on an old glibc].
  - **What changed** [S]: (1) `native/build-native.sh`'s Linux `LINK_EXTRA` is `-static-libgcc -pthread -Wl,--no-undefined` (`:306`; the reasons in its comment, `:267-290`). `-pthread` versions the six (`pthread_create@GLIBC_2.2.5` and so on) and makes `libpthread.so.0` NEEDED below glibc 2.34, while the highest need stays GLIBC_2.17; on glibc 2.43 the .so is byte-identical with or without it [M]. `--no-undefined` makes a missing library a link error. It holds for this JNI library, because `jnlua.c` reaches the JVM only through the JNIEnv tables [S][M]. Not `-ldl`: the .so imports none of libdl's functions (`dlopen`, `dlsym`, `dlclose`, `dlerror`); its one `dl_` import, `dl_iterate_phdr` (from libgcc's unwinder), is libc's, and `libdl.so.2` is not NEEDED [M: `readelf --dyn-syms -W` on both release .so files, 2026-10-20, each listing `dl_iterate_phdr@GLIBC_2.2.5` as its only `dl` import; NEEDED in `bench/runs/2026-10-19-glibc/logs/build-alma/glibc.txt`; I: that libgcc's unwinder is the importer]. (2) Step 6 gains an ELF import gate, Linux only, between `# BEGIN ELF IMPORT GATE` and `# END ELF IMPORT GATE` (`:1883-1984`). It reads the library with `readelf --dyn-syms -W` and `readelf -V -W`, whose output reads the same under binutils 2.30 and 2.46; `objdump -T`'s does not [M]. It requires that readelf listed some undefined symbol, since on a truncated file readelf lists none and exits 0. It fails any non-weak undefined symbol that has no version, naming each one. And it reads the version needs (`.gnu.version_r`) for the highest GLIBC_x.y, comparing versions component by component. With `OCLJ_GLIBC_MAX` set (preflight refuses a malformed value, `:351-364`), a need above that limit, or outside the GLIBC_x.y family (GLIBC_PRIVATE, or GCC_x.y from libgcc_s), is a BUILD FAIL. The failure lists the needs, their libraries and up to 12 imports, and nothing is collected into `dist`. With it unset, the gate reports the verdict against the release floor (`GLIBC_FLOOR=2.28`, `:353`) and the build goes on, so a development build on a newer distribution finishes. The header's EXTERNAL INPUTS and USAGE (`:112-143`) and PREREQUISITES 7 (`:2050-2067`) name AlmaLinux 8 as the release host and give its command. (These `build-native.sh` cites are the file as of 2026-10-20, md5 `f3dc7167`, re-derived by content.) (3) `build.gradle.kts`'s `verifyModAssets` reads every packaged `.so` itself, with no binutils. It reads the ELF64 header, the section headers and the one `.gnu.version_r`, walking its Verneed and Vernaux chains by their next fields; the chains must end where `sh_info` and `vn_cnt` say, and every read is bounds-checked. It refuses a need above `glibcFloor = "2.28"` (`:74`), a need outside GLIBC_x.y, or a file it cannot parse, naming the reason and the rebuild command. A .so that passes logs `needs at most GLIBC_x.y (the glibc floor is GLIBC_2.28)` (`:223-373`). It does not refuse unversioned imports, which only build-native.sh checks: the trial .so passes all four gradle checks [M]. The floor lives in both files, so change them together. (4) Every Linux test link takes `-pthread -ldl`. That is `TEST_EXTRA` in `run.sh`, `run-security.sh`, `run-mem.sh`, `run-wd.sh`, `run-vgc.sh`, `run-pack.sh`, `run-bitop.sh` and `run-penalty.sh` (which had `-ldl` alone), and in `negative-control.sh`, whose six links had no tail. `serializer/Makefile`'s Linux `LIBS` is `-lm -pthread -ldl`; it already linked, and the change gives every test link one spelling. (5) The harness runs on Linux: the row above.

Both causes were in the test, not in the product, and the follow-up fixed them in `test/native/negative-control.sh` (md5 `7d191b40` to `452d869b`) [M]:
       - **notimer** sabotaged `lj52_wd_fire`, the Win32 timer-queue callback, which Linux compiles out (`native/lj52shim.c:2016-2034`, under `#else /* LJ52_WD_WIN32 */`). The "sabotaged" Linux build was the canonical one, and it still fired the deadline: W2 interrupted at 55.5 ms, wd 35/0. The arm now picks its sabotage by host: on Linux it removes the condvar thread's only call into the VM, `lj52_wd_inject(M);` in `lj52_wd_thread` (`:1978`), so the thread counts the fire and never delivers it. A preprocessor guard stops the run with SETUP FAIL (exit 2) when the host compiles the sabotaged text out; with the two hosts' texts swapped it does so on Windows, AlmaLinux 8 and Ubuntu. As written, the arm is caught on all three (exit 99: the test's alarm ends the run). A preprocessor census of all 51 sabotaged copies found notimer the only arm whose `lj52shim.c` text Linux compiles out; `dropmode`, whose sabotage is a macro in `lj52shim.h` acting in `security_test.c`, was outside its view, and it passes in AlmaLinux 8.
       - **The four allocator arms** (`rawverdict`, `nolook`, `refusalkeeps`, `noarm`) failed because AlmaLinux 8's `/bin/sh`, bash 4.4.20 in POSIX mode, keeps an assignment written before a shell function call after the call returns; Git Bash's bash 5.3.15 and Ubuntu's dash do not. `MEMTEST_CFLAGS="-DW16_N=8 -DW16R_N=8" sabotage_mem nohyst ...` therefore left every later mem arm, 23 of them, sweeping 8 caps (0 to 448 B) instead of 96 (0 to 6080 B, one 6 KB step of the fill), and those four arms' W16-family refusals first land past +448 B. At 96 caps all four fail exactly their expected checks on AlmaLinux 8, Ubuntu and Windows, with the same first failing offsets, counts and request sizes; at 8 caps Windows misses them exactly as AlmaLinux 8 did. The fix sets and clears the variable on lines of its own and clears it again at the top of section 4. A new check, 4.31, reads each arm's own W16 line and requires the arms that did not sweep 96 caps to be exactly `nohyst:8 unbounded:8`; it fails on the chain's own Alma work dir and on a value leaked from the caller on Windows.
       - With both fixes, the full script passes 57 of 57 (the 56 plus 4.31) in AlmaLinux 8 (10 min 8 s), in Ubuntu (9 min 41 s, its first full run there) and on Windows (11 min 8 s), with the same RESULT labels on all three; the old text on the same objects gives 51/5 in AlmaLinux 8 and 55/1 (notimer only) in Ubuntu [M: scratch `claude-tmp/oclj/glibc/followup/fix-negctl/run-{alma,ubuntu,win}-new` and `run-{alma,ubuntu}-old`; the investigations in `inv-wd` and `inv-mem`]. Those runs used mirrors of the release-identical scratch builds (in AlmaLinux 8 `glibc/main/alma-fixed`, whose .so files equal the release's but whose `libluajit.a` md5 differs), not `build/native` itself. The main session then closed that gap: the fixed script on the real builds passed 57 of 57 on Windows (`build/native`), in AlmaLinux 8 (the release `build/native`) and in Ubuntu (the development build), and the serializer suite, the fixed for-in test included, passed in full on all three [M: `bench/runs/2026-10-19-glibc/logs/final/`]. The expected count is now 57 RESULT lines on every host.

The AlmaLinux 8 trial .so (six unversioned pthread imports), which every gradle check passed before, is refused; the release .so is accepted with "110 imports, every non-weak one versioned". An adversarial verifier found no false accept and no false refuse against readelf over 3219 real files [M: `bench/runs/2026-10-20-gradle-imports/`].
    4. The gate's awk ran under gawk in both distros, and the review ran its text under mawk too (Ubuntu's stock awk): 14 of 14 verdicts as wanted under each [M: `design/review-results.json`, find:build]; busybox awk is untested [I: it uses POSIX awk only].
    5. **The serializer's for-in suite failed once in AlmaLinux 8** (`logs/suite-alma/ser-test.log`: `FAIL diag/4-identity -- tests/forin.lua:564: two in-process blobs of one shape differ in length`), and passed in 12 reruns there. A flake of the test on every platform, not a Linux or a serializer difference [M]: the unmodified test failed 20 of 2000 fresh processes in AlmaLinux 8, 18 of 2000 in Ubuntu and 26 of 2000 on Windows. It had two causes, both in the test. (a) The JIT's hot counters are shared by PC hash, so in about 1 process in 100 a trace starts in the identity shape and stitches across `coroutine.yield`, which leaves a continuation frame under the yield: a different VM state, whose blob is 20 bytes longer, written faithfully, and restoring in a fresh process to the same results. (b) The frame-link window's locator accepted two windows when the PC's low byte was 4 (`2 candidates`), and it could accept a one-ulp difference in a real double as the link. The fix is in `serializer/tests/forin.lua` only (md5 `6bf14b5b` to `41b0fd3f`): the identity shape runs with `jit.off(chunk, true)`, and the window must read as a GC64 address in both blobs. No serializer, fixture or wire change. With it, the final file passed 1000 of 1000 fresh processes in AlmaLinux 8, 500 of 500 in Ubuntu and 500 of 500 on Windows, and the same code (comments aside) 2000 of 2000 on each; under forced tracing (`hotloop=1`) the final file passes 20 of 20 on each platform, where the unfixed test fails 20 of 20 and the final file without the JIT-off fails 19, 19 and 20; a real identity break (a changed value, a changed shape, a pair built over different tables) still fails 20 of 20 on each; and the full `run-forin.sh` passes on all three [M: scratch `claude-tmp/oclj/glibc/followup/inv-forin` and `fix-forin`]. `serializer/README.md`'s known limitation now also names a continuation frame's two raw slot words, its `lj_cont_*` code address and its `contpc`, inert like the frame link; whether to write nil in their place is in the M5 row.

Five commits since changed the shim, the kernel or the LuaJIT patches with no recorded in-game test: d705515 (stage A), f21a64b (stage B), 85faa0e (stage C, kernel site 13), 3186e87 (THE WINDOW), a4994e6 (the reserve's size, the errmem clamp). The GTNH kernel with site 13 (`1d12c322`) is built and postflight-checked ([bench/results-wall-2026-10-04.md](../bench/results-wall-2026-10-04.md):550-552) and has no recorded boot: the harness patches ocelot-brain's `machine.lua` instead (`test/native/smoke-test.sh:502-504`), and the last in-game run had `089dcbde`. The installed `mods/ocluajit-a4994e6.jar` carries HEAD's natives (DLL `45eb443b`, .so `45b25422`) and kernel `1d12c322` byte for byte (verified 2026-10-10). A T10 covers: (1) a power-cycled machine boots to the shell (in-game-tests.md:16-21: a restored machine keeps the kernel it was saved with); (2) on the smallest stick, 256 KB in the instance's `ramSizes` (`config/OpenComputers.cfg`, verified 2026-10-10), a program fills memory, catches "not enough memory", drops its data and carries on, a recovery the capacity probe saw refused in 12 of our 60 runs on 2026-10-03 and in none of 60 after stage B (the collector-at-the-wall row); (3) a save and reload while the machine holds data near its cap, the path the fresh-record rule covers (`native/lj52shim.c:986-995`). The errmem clamp has no known in-game shape; its evidence is the census (the unwinder-crash row). **Status 2026-10-13:** a sixth change for T10, Value disposal ([bench/results-dispose-2026-10-13.md](../bench/results-dispose-2026-10-13.md); the Value disposal row), verified in the harness and not yet committed. Its item: (4) in the pack, where `maxHandles=64` (OC's default is 16), open handles through `component.filesystem.open` until an open is refused (the 65th), drop them unclosed, let the kernel collect (it runs a full collect every 10 resumes, `build/native/kernel/machine.lua:1631-1636` in kernel `1d12c322`, and on LuaJIT a dropped Value is disposed in the second full cycle after its proxy dies), then open again: it must succeed, where on aa12418's natives it stays refused until reboot (the harness's fail-first: `too_many_open_handles`). That is the first run of the product's install path, which the harness reaches only through its twin `OcljArch.scala`; the save's second `COLLECT` has no in-game shape planned, and its evidence is harness `gc-3` and its sabotage. The jar to test is not built from a commit yet: `build/libs/ocluajit-aa12418-master+aa124186c8-dirty.jar` (md5 `c1569833`) is a dirty-tree build and is not installed; build T10's jar from the commit. The natives it must carry are additive DLL `6445f2e4` and .so `0deb53ab`. The installed jar predates the fix (its DLL `45eb443b` exports neither Value-disposal native), so (4) on it would be the before-run. The fresh-record rule (3) cites is now at `native/lj52shim.c:1305-1314`, and the harness's kernel patch cited above at `test/native/smoke-test.sh:518-520`. **Status 2026-10-22:** the jar to test is `build/libs/ocluajit-19a7806.jar` (md5 `1e02e032`), built from the clean tree of 19a7806, which contains every commit this row lists (`git merge-base --is-ancestor`, checked 2026-10-22); it carries DLL `9ac03aeb`, .so `f91700bd` and kernel `1d12c322`. The bitwise test written on 2026-10-22 took the next free name, T11 ([in-game-tests.md](in-game-tests.md)), because this row holds T10; it runs on the same jar, and its setup power-cycles the machine, so a T11 session also records this row's item (1).
