The refusal did the power-cycle's work: with no saved kernel to restore, the computer's next start ran this jar's kernel `1d12c322` [I], and it booted OpenOS [I: the results file exists] and ran T11's LuaJIT arm, which passed [M]. So kernel `1d12c322` now has a recorded in-game boot, and item (1) is done. T11 is not a per-fix test, so the row stays open for items (2)-(4), still unrun.

## v2 — JIT on

The headline release: enable the JIT (`luaopen_jit`, then hide `jit`/`debug` from the sandbox) and make the watchdog trace-proof. **Status 2026-10-03:** all three are done. `luaopen_jit` runs at every newstate (`lj52shim.c:1852`); the whitelist sandbox copies no `jit` and only OC's restricted `debug` table (`build/native/kernel/machine.lua:1037`); and the watchdog is trace-proof (the deadline-hook row in v1, and the adopt-the-watchdog row below). What is left of v2 is the hard-abort backstop, `jit.opt` sizing, the in-game comparison with the Lua 5.3 CPU and the benchmark suite's own upkeep, the platforms, the polish, and two upstream reports whose filing waits on a decision. **2026-10-10:** the platforms row is no longer v2's: it moved to Parked (macOS and linux-aarch64 unsupported for now).

- [x] **JIT x persist interaction — MEASURED 2026-09-19, and it is not the risk it read as.** `persist()` calls `lj_trace_flushall` whenever it reaches a thread containing a generic-for loop, and an idle OpenOS is parked inside one essentially always: the harness's `m2` shows every save flushing all of it (mcode 196608 → 0 B, 290 traces → 0). The open question was the cost on a machine that KEEPS RUNNING after the save, at Minecraft's autosave cadence (every 900 ticks = 45 s; OC persists a machine on every chunk save). New milestone `m4-persist-and-continue-under-load`: five in-place saves on the running machine with the mandelbrot encore hot, sampling the first run after each flush (cold) and the one after it (re-warmed). Three runs: warm best 0.1507/0.1451/0.1480 s → cold mean 0.1528/0.1591/0.1568 s (1.01x/1.10x/1.06x) → re-warmed 0.1601/0.1634/0.1602 s; **cold minus re-warmed is −3 to −7 ms inside a 4–9 ms sd, i.e. the first run after a flush is not measurably slower than the runs after it**; mandelbrot's ~50–100 traces come back inside its first 150 ms run (46 → 56 → 95 traces across saves). The `jit=off` control has nothing to flush (mcode 0 before and after every save) and shows the same shape (0.9308 s warm best, cold 0.9030, re-warmed 0.9127: −10 ms), so the cold-vs-warm-best gap of +2…+14 ms on the JIT arm is mean-versus-minimum sampling, not the flush. Persist itself is ~7 ms of wall time. **Cost per autosave on a compute-bound machine: bounded by ~10 ms and not resolvable from noise, ≤0.03% of a 45 s interval; the "may never warm up" scenario does not occur.** Not measured: a workload with many cold-start traces (an OS event loop with many distinct paths) pays the recompile across many short runs instead of one — the same total, spread out. (Moot since 2026-09-22: the persist-side flush is gone, below, so an autosave no longer throws traces away.) **The persist-side flush can still be removed outright, and cheaply:** the only reason it exists is that the scan reads the loop head and its ITERL from the prototype's bytecode, which a root trace patches in place (JLOOP/JITERL with the trace number in D); the original instruction is kept in `GCtrace.startins`, and `lj_bcwrite.c` already reads through it exactly this way when dumping a prototype. A read-through helper makes `persist()` free of any JIT side effect and deletes its "cannot flush inside a GC hook" refusal; the restore-side flush stays (one-time, into a VM that is cold anyway). **SHIPPED 2026-09-22** (serializer hash `8c5a1168`): `elj_bc_orig` reads a JLOOP/JITERL/JFORL head through `GCtrace.startins` after checking the trace owns the slot, and refuses (never skips) a compiled head with no trace; every bytecode read in the scan goes through it; the persist-side flush block and `Info.flushed` are gone; the restore-side flush stays. Fail-first m3 case: the yielded-in loop's head is JIT-patched at persist time (asserted via `jit.attach`), and "every trace compiled in it is still there after persist" reads 0 of 1 on the old exe, 1 of 1 on the new. **And removing it exposed a LuaJIT bug the flush had been masking for the whole project — the next row.**
- [x] **A RE-RUN PROGRAM INHERITS A DEAD ONE'S TRACE-ABORT HISTORY AND IS BLACKLISTED TO THE INTERPRETER BY ITS ~9TH RUN — a LuaJIT defect, found 2026-09-22 by the harness the moment the per-save flush stopped masking it; cured the same day with a LuaJIT patch on the build copy.** Symptom: `m4-persist-and-continue-under-load` on the new native — saves 1–3 at 1.0x, then after save 4 the mandelbrot encore at **0.9–1.0 s instead of 0.15 s, permanently**, the trace number frozen. Bisect (five serialised harness runs swapping only the DLL, plus two scratch builds mixing old/new serializer and shim): the serializer's flush removal, not the shim, not the diagnostic drain; and the slow sample came one encore run AFTER a save with no persist in between, so the persist was a bystander. Mechanism, established standalone with a 5 s `luajit.exe` probe and no serializer in the loop (`scratchpad/bump/bisect/penalty-probe*`): LuaJIT's trace-abort penalty cache `J->penalty[64]` (`lj_jit.h:303-312`, `PENALTY_MIN` 72, `PENALTY_MAX` 60000) is keyed by the raw BCIns ADDRESS of a loop head (`penalty_pc`, `lj_trace.c:391-414`) and is never scrubbed when a prototype dies (`lj_func_freeproto` is a bare `lj_mem_free`; the only other writer is `lj_trace_flushall`'s memset). Our machine heap was CRT `realloc`/`free` (`lj52_alloc`; until 2026-10-02 — each machine's own `lj_alloc` arena since, which reuses a freed block too, as the upstream report reproduces), which hands a freed prototype's block straight back to the next same-size `load()`, so a FRESH prototype inherits the DEAD one's slot; each run's ordinary nested-loop aborts (`LJ_TRERR_LLEAVE`/`LINNER`) double it (72·2^10 > 60000 on the 11th abort at one address); `blacklist_pc` then rewrites the fresh program's loop heads to `ILOOP`/`IFORL` on their first abort and never lowers the slot, so every later re-load at that address is interpreted from its first abort. Probe: address constant runs 2–11, run 11 blacklisted (0.538 s vs 0.10), run 14 blacklisted on its first abort. **Correction 2026-09-22 (later the same day): it is NOT specific to our allocator.** With the reproducer's own churn kept out of the way, a pristine upstream build (no CHECKHOOK, no compat, LuaJIT's own `lj_alloc`) reused the freed prototype's block on all 19 re-loads in 3 of 4 runs and blacklisted at run 9 (0.535 s vs 0.104 s); the earlier "1 in 20" came from a probe that allocated inside its `jit.attach` callback. Upstream report with a self-contained pure-Lua reproducer (`bench/luajit-penalty-repro.lua`) and the fix as a diff against upstream `lj_func.c`: [research/luajit-penalty-cache-report.md](research/luajit-penalty-cache-report.md); `jit.flush()` between runs (= the old per-save memset) never blacklists across 14 runs. **In-game this was live all along**: the flush reset the cache once per autosave, so any program re-run ≥ 9 times between two saves — a shell loop, a REPL script, an OS reloading a program — was already ending up interpreted. **Cure** (`native/luajit/patch-penalty-scrub.sh`, applied by `build-native.sh` to a PRISTINE re-copy of `lj_func.c` on every build and platform, asserted by content before make and by `lj_func.o` timestamp after; the pinned checkout stays untouched): in `lj_func_freeproto`, null every penalty slot whose pc lies in `[proto_bc(pt), proto_bc(pt)+sizebc)` — 64 pointer compares per prototype death; a live prototype's legitimate penalties are untouched. **Gate** `test/native/run-penalty.sh` (a CRT-allocator host re-loading `bench/oc/mandelbrot.lua` 40 times with a full GC between): unpatched `libluajit.a` — first blacklist at run 11 (visit 9 of one address), 5.97x, slowest 8.45x, 3 of 6 checks FAIL; patched — 40/40 clean, slowest 1.43x, zero slots outliving their prototype, and the two positive controls (a loop that aborts on every record still reads `ILOOP` / `IFORL` after 400 000 iterations) pass on both. Harness after the cure: m4 **1.07–1.09x across all five saves with traces surviving**, 47/0 in warn and ignore mode. `_OCLJ_JITSTATS` gained a fifth value, `traces_live` (non-NULL slots), because `freetrace-1` cannot tell "no recording" from "every recording aborts" — it read 468 frozen while the loop was interpreted. Reviewed by three lenses; the address-range test, `G2J` validity during close, and the idempotency of the patch step were the objections, the last one closed by the pristine re-copy (a hand-mangled block is now overwritten, shown failing first). **In-game confirmed 2026-09-22** (T5 in [in-game-tests.md](in-game-tests.md): a compute script re-run 15 times from the shell and 15 times from a yielding REPL loop, same checksum and 5–8 ms every run — compiled speed throughout). Worth reporting upstream with the probe; also a fair question for the stock `lj_alloc` under a long-lived server, where the 1-in-20 address reuse compounds. (**2026-10-03:** the report is still a draft, tracked in the upstream-reports row. The "1-in-20" was withdrawn by the correction above: a pristine `lj_alloc` reused the block on all 19 re-loads in 3 of 4 runs, and machines have run on `lj_alloc` arenas, with the scrub, since 2026-10-02.)
- [x] Adopt the prototype-validated watchdog: no standing hook; Java watchdog thread injects `lua_sethook(count=1)` at deadline; `LUAJIT_ENABLE_CHECKHOOK` build so compiled traces take the exit; machine.lua's count=1 re-arm escalation for pcall-swallowing code **Done -- verified 2026-10-03:** there is no standing hook. `_OCLJ_WATCHDOG.arm/disarm` (lj52shim.c:1850-1940) runs at the kernel's three resume sites (machine.lua:751, :883, :1625), with CHECKHOOK built and probed (build-native.sh:242, :539-555). Three things differ from the plan: the injector is a native timer (a Win32 timer-queue timer, or a Linux condvar thread) rather than a Java thread; it ORs the count bit in atomically (lj52shim.c:1589) rather than calling `lua_sethook`; and machine.lua's count=1 re-arm is deleted (kernel site 12), its escalation now carried by the native's 50 ms re-fire (`LJ52_WD_REFIRE_MS`, :1472). Gated by wd_test W1-W13, race_test, harness k1/k5/k6 and in-game T4.
- [ ] Hard-abort path: state destruction after resume returns; thread-abandonment backstop for the residual native-code case, with server-log diagnostics **Status 2026-10-03:** the state-destruction half needs no code of its own. Past the grace, the native's 50 ms count=1 re-fire raises on every instruction, so the resume returns its error, and OC's crash path closes the state: `crash` (Machine.scala:304) calls `stop()`, which schedules the close (:277-283), and the scheduled `close()` (:939) calls `architecture.close()` (:946), i.e. `NativeLuaArchitecture.close` → `lua.close()`. Harness k6 and in-game T4 show it. The abandonment backstop and its diagnostics do not exist: there is no per-machine runner thread and no abandon code in src/ or native/. So a C builtin that never returns, such as backtracking in the native matcher on a string under 500 bytes (kernel machine.lua:138, :682-683), still holds an OC executor thread, exactly as on stock (research/wd-blind-spots.md 1a). **Status 2026-10-10:** Unchanged at HEAD: no runner thread or abandon code in `src/` or `native/`. find/match (kernel `:544-545`) and gmatch (`:599-600`) take the native matcher under the same 500-byte split. By reading OC-GTNH's `Machine.scala` (1.12.55-GTNH sources), a resume wedged this way also blocks the server thread at that machine's next save: `save` (`:821`) waits on the monitor `run()` holds across `runThreaded` (`:983-1000`), on stock too. Not run. The close half's evidence, k6 4/4 and T4, dates from 2026-09-22 on the 12-site kernel (`docs/in-game-tests.md:155-161`; T4 on DLL `49ead6cc`), before the arena, the collector at the wall, the errmem patch and site 13; `bench/runs` records no k6 run since, and one harness run with `OCLJ_PROBE=grace` would refresh it. For the matcher case alone, `SHORT_STRING = 0` is the smaller change (research/wd-blind-spots.md 1a recommends it or the hard abort; the builtin-upvalue sweep row proposes it for gmatch); its cost on short strings is unmeasured, and choosing it or stage 4 (`docs/watchdog.md:39`) is a user decision.
- [ ] `jit.opt` tuning for many small computers (`maxmcode`, `maxtrace` scaled to installed RAM). **Now with a number, 2026-09-22:** since saves no longer flush, trace metadata (GCtrace, IR, snapshots — charged to the RAM cap, unlike machine code) stays resident until LuaJIT's own self-flush at 1000 live traces or a full 2 MB mcode reserve. Measured with the new `OCLJ_RAM_TIER` knob and the print-only `mem-1` milestone, bundle vs pre-bump, one run per cell: at the 256 KB tier the idle OpenOS shell shows **free 102–131 KB after the first save vs 186–224 KB on the pre-bump** (which had just flushed), i.e. ~460 resident traces at ~0.65 KB real each ≈ 90 KB as the sandbox sees it at ramScale 3.0; at 384 KB, 229–261 vs 350–375 KB; flat across the five m4 saves (+3–4 KB), and nothing failed for memory at either tier. Before the first save the two natives are within noise, because the pre-bump's traces were resident too until a save threw them away. So the bundle's cost is exactly what a save used to give back, and it lands hardest on the small tiers. **The first answer shipped 2026-09-22: traces are flushed under memory pressure.** Not from the allocator — the emergency collector never calls anything, by design — but at the one safe point the shim owns: `watchdog.arm`, which the kernel calls on the Lua thread before every resume. Predicate: when an armed emergency cycle is PROVEN complete and headroom is STILL under the watermark (garbage alone did not help, so resident trace metadata is what is reclaimable), `lj52_gc_pressure` raises `gc_flush_wanted`; the next `arm` consumes it, sums the resident metadata by `lj_trace_free`'s own formula, calls `luaJIT_setmode(FLUSH)` (guarded: `HOOK_GC` is tested first because `luaJIT_setmode` RAISES under it rather than returning), and re-arms the cycle so the unlinked `GCtrace` objects are swept at the very next checkpoint. Never raised merely on arming — a small machine idling near its watermark arms constantly and would lose the JIT on every resume; at the 256 KB tier the idle shell shows `arms=162 trace_flushes=0`. `_OCLJ_GCSTATS` appends `trace_flushes`, `flush_wanted`, `flush_refusals`, `flush_bytes`. Gates: `mem_test` (`run-mem.sh`, 26/26; 7 fail first on the old object: the flag and counters absent, 40 traces still live after the arm) and harness `mem-2-pressure-flushes-traces` at the 256 KB tier — a program holds 503 KB live with sandbox free down to 63 KB and keeps computing: `trace_flushes 0 → 81`, `traces_live 459 → 0` while it runs, program survives; on the previous DLL the counter is absent → FAIL; 50/0 at both tiers, the control 49 PASS + mem-2 FAIL, nothing else moved. **The documented degradation:** a machine whose LIVE data alone is past the watermark flushes at every resume that has a trace (81 flushes in ~5 s here) — the JIT is effectively off for it, which beats "not enough memory", and `trace_flushes` makes it visible. Scaling `maxtrace` with installed RAM stays open as the second answer, now optional. **Status 2026-10-03:** the pressure flush is in the code and gated: `lj52_gc_flushtraces` (lj52shim.c:1428), consumed in `watchdog.arm` (:1874), tested by mem_test and harness mem-2. The item's own subject is not done. Nothing sets `maxtrace` or `maxmcode`: the shim only reads `JIT_P_maxmcode` (:2009), and `OCLJ_JITOPT` is a token build-native.sh:313 rejects. So every machine runs LuaJIT's defaults, 1000 traces and 2048 KB of mcode, whatever its RAM. The per-tier figures above predate the 2026-10-02 arena. Its gate run read tier-one free 131 KB lower (results-allocator-2026-10-02.md, one run each, not examined). **Status 2026-10-10:** Takes over the machine-code-blindness row, merged here. Machine code is `VirtualAlloc`'d and never passes `allocf` (`lj_mcode.c:86-90`), so the RAM cap never sees it: on the shipped DLL `45eb443b` (the installed jar's, verified 2026-10-10) at the default threehalf tier (1024 KB, raw cap 2 052 360 B) it reads 128 KB after boot, 640 KB after the seven-benchmark suite and 704 KB after a save, against a 2048 KB `maxmcode`, and mem-2's 21 pressure flushes took it to 64 KB (`bench/runs/2026-10-05-reserve/logs/runsF/full/fullF-additive-on.log:92`, `:161`, `:169`, `:262`, `:266`). The flush predicate is now headroom under HALF the watermark (85faa0e, `LJ52_GC_FLUSHSHIFT`), never on the proof of a cycle the flush armed (`lj52shim.c:1376`). The per-tier figures above were taken at the harness's old ramScale 3.0 (OC's 1.8 since bd302f2) and with compiled code in kernelMemory (none since 85faa0e); no archived MEM-1 read is below threehalf, and a 192 KB machine idling on stage C flushed once in 3 of 16 runs, at 103-116 traces holding 83-86 KB (`bench/results-wall-2026-10-04.md:601-610`). Nothing sets `maxtrace`, `maxmcode` or `sizemcode` (grep at HEAD). A per-machine rule needs `sizemcode` as well, since one 64 KB area is granted whatever `maxmcode` says (`lj_mcode.c:391-394`), and must ignore the save and load windows, which set the cap to `Integer.MAX_VALUE` (`LuaJITArchitecture.java:499`, `:574`). The trade-off is the user's: reaching `maxmcode` flushes every trace (`lj_trace.c:656-661`), so a tight bound costs small machines JIT time, unmeasured.
- [ ] Benchmark suite in-game: compute-bound (SHA-256, deflate, mandelbrot, pathfinding) and component-bound (terminal I/O) vs Lua 5.3 CPU; publish honest numbers **Status 2026-10-03:** the game has a compute column for mandelbrot, matmul and binarytrees (T7-T9, `bench/oc/ingame-ladder.lua`; T9 0.0657 / 0.0862 / 0.2521 s). It is measured against plain LuaJIT and the capped host, not the Lua 5.3 CPU. The only stock-architecture comparison is the harness's PUC 5.2 cell A (bench/results-in-machine-phase1-2026-09-04.md). Not done: a Lua 5.3 CPU run in game, SHA-256 in game (left out of the runner), deflate, pathfinding and terminal I/O (no such benchmark in bench/), and a player-facing write-up. **Status 2026-10-10:** The 5.3 column is the one a player meets: GTNH crafts CPUs as Lua 5.3 by default (verified 2026-10-10: the pack's `config/OpenComputers.cfg:213` defaultLua53=true; OC `application.conf:232`), so cell A's PUC 5.2 is a non-default baseline. Cell A is also not the only stock comparison: 11 pinned stock PUC 5.2 suites from 2026-10-03 to 10-05 sit in `bench/runs`, none written up; on the final build mandelbrot reads 0.8149 s there against our 0.0647 s (`bench/runs/2026-10-05-reserve/logs/runsF/full/fullF-stock.log`, `fullF-additive-on.log`). By reading, the runner needs no change for a 5.3 CPU: its three benchmarks return their reference CHECKs on standalone Lua 5.3.6 (`bench/vendor/lua-5.3.6`, checked 2026-10-10), and it reads `jit` only through `rawget` (`bench/oc/ingame-ladder.lua:38`). **Status 2026-10-22:** a first Lua 5.3 column exists in procedure: in-game-tests.md T11 times five bitwise loops (table-driven CRC32, xorshift32, byte packing, `x | 0`, `select`) on the Lua 5.3 CPU and ours with one file, `bench/oc/ingame-bitops.lua`; in the harness Lua 5.3 read 11-24x ours (medians; `bench/runs/2026-10-22-t11/`). Its LuaJIT arm ran in game the same day: 0.62 to 4.94 ns per operation, every row within 14% of the harness's LuaJIT arm but `select` (+22%; `bench/runs/2026-10-22-t11-game/`). Its Lua 5.3 arm did not run, so the game still has no Lua 5.3 column, and the suite's other benchmarks are unchanged. **Status 2026-10-23:** the game has its first Lua 5.3 column. ## v3 — Optional data persistence

*(May be subsumed by Track P below if it succeeds; kept as the fallback.)* **Superseded -- 2026-10-03:** Track P succeeded (in game T1–T3), so every row below is superseded; the section is kept for the record.

- [x] Explicit opt-in API (e.g. `computer.setPersistData(blob)` / boot signal carrying the blob back), `string.buffer`-based encoding with a cycle-handling wrapper **Superseded -- 2026-10-03:** Track P made this fallback unnecessary. OC's own `PersistenceAPI` persists the whole machine through `eris_lj.c` (`LuaJITArchitecture.java:475-523` save, `:565-642` load; harness `f1`-`f3`, in game T1-T3, 2026-09-17/19), so a program's state survives a save with no explicit blob. No `setPersistData`-style API exists in the tree.
- [x] External-file storage following OC's SaveHandler pattern (large blobs must stay out of chunk NBT — see OpenPython/OC-Wasm precedents) **Superseded -- 2026-10-03:** the full-state blob already stays out of chunk NBT. `LuaJITArchitecture.java:518-523` hands it to OC's `SaveHandler.scheduleSave`, and `:584-588` reads it back with `SaveHandler.load`. That path writes `<save>/<savePath>/state/<dim>/<chunkX>.<chunkZ>/<address>_kernel` (OC `SaveHandler.scala:51-61`, `:75-78`), and T2 recorded a 183139 B `_kernel` file there.
- [x] Example OpenOS service showing the "save data, re-run code" pattern for automation controllers **Superseded -- 2026-10-03:** with transparent persistence, a running controller resumes where it stopped instead of re-running. Harness `f2-restore-same-vm` shows the same boot nonce and `f3-restore-counter-continues` shows the counter advancing; in game T1-T3 show the same. The opt-in API this example would demonstrate was never built (the opt-in API row).

## Track P — full transparent persistence

Feasibility study complete and positive: [persistence-study.md](persistence-study.md). Goal: an Eris-API-compatible serializer (`eris_lj.c` sidecar compiled into the pinned LuaJIT build) so OC's `PersistenceAPI`/machine.lua machinery works unchanged and LuaJIT computers persist like the stock CPUs. ~3–4k LOC C, months part-time; runs in parallel with v1/v2 (M0–M2 don't depend on the JNI bridge). **Status 2026-10-03:** the goal is met and Track P ships (M0–M4.5 below; in game T1–T3). Two details came out differently: `serializer/eris_lj.c` is compiled on its own and linked into `libjnluajit52` beside `jnlua.o` and `lj52shim.o` (`native/build-native.sh:722-734`), not into the LuaJIT build; and `machine.lua` is patched where persistence needed it (the shell-fill recipes, and M4.5's two snapshot walks). What remains is M5.

- [x] Feasibility study with adversarial verification (no structural blocker; suspended coroutines are pure Lua-stack state; continuation set closed at 9 entries; cont_stitch aux slot handled)
- [x] **M0 — validation spike** *(done 2026-09-01)*: read-only frame-chain walker, [../prototype/framewalk](../prototype/framewalk/). All 7 invariants hold across 8 suspended threads in 7 frame shapes (plain yield, pcall/xpcall, `__index`/`__concat` metamethods, vararg chains with open upvalues, nested coroutines, JIT-hot loop). Two schema corrections found: the `cont_stitch` saved trace is at **framebase − 5** (not the provisionally assumed slot), and frame-type decoding must test `frame_islua()` before `frame_typep()` or pcall frames are silently mislabeled.
- [x] M0b — rerun the walker against real OC kernel states once the JNI bridge exists (v1), to confirm machine.lua's actual suspend shapes match the catalog **Superseded -- 2026-10-03:** the walker was not rerun. Its frame catalog became the serializer's thread writer (`serializer/eris_lj.c:1518-1574`), which refuses by name any frame outside it (unknown or hook continuation, unsupported frame type, interior C frame). Real `machine.lua` states pass through that writer without a refusal on every harness save (`f1-persist-blob`, `stk-1`, `fi-1`, `m4`) and every in-game save (T1-T3, T6), and the kernel's frame chain is recorded in [research/m3-oc-shapes.md](research/m3-oc-shapes.md) section 1.
- [x] **M1 — data serializer** *(done 2026-09-01)*: [../serializer](../serializer/) — tables/strings/numbers with shared-ref map, cycles, metatables, perms-at-every-node, spkey literal semantics; fingerprinted header + CRC32; 84 tests pass. Survived a four-lens adversarial review that found and fixed **two crash bugs** (a SIGSEGV from an unvalidated permanent type byte, and an uncatchable C-stack overflow from an unclamped `maxrec`) plus a silent write-only-save-data defect; all ten findings have regression tests. See [../serializer/README.md](../serializer/README.md).
- [x] **M2 — code** *(done 2026-09-01)*: Lua closures via `lj_bcwrite`/`lua_loadx`, upvalue identity through `lua_upvalueid`/`lua_upvaluejoin`, function environments, and the full spkey function protocol; 55 tests (M1 still 83). `debug.upvalueid` equality preserved across a restore. A second four-lens review found a **critical** ordering defect — the reader published an upvalue's owner after reading its value, so the ordinary module pattern produced write-only blobs — plus deterministic dumps, an inert `debug` setting, and a stack-budget correction; all fixed with regression tests. Prototype dedup deferred (documented). See [../serializer/README.md](../serializer/README.md).
- [x] **M3 — suspended coroutines** *(done 2026-09-01)*: [../serializer](../serializer/) — suspended threads round-trip and resume exactly where they left off, including open upvalues aliasing live frames, continuation frames, nested coroutines and the OC kernel shape. 71 tests (M1 82, M2 55), plus a 15-case cross-process suite for for-in replay. A verification+review workflow re-checked the ten design claims M3 was built on (one refuted, one materially corrected) and found **16 defects, 4 critical** — including a heap overflow reachable from an ordinary deep coroutine with no tampering. All fixed; see [../serializer/README.md](../serializer/README.md). A follow-up audit verified every fix against the code and caught one regression (the error-dead-thread fix had turned a loud refusal into write-only save data — a coroutine that dies by error does not close its open upvalues); fixed. `ipairs` is solved host-side by sweeping builtins' function-valued upvalues into perms (**corrected 2026-10-03:** solved in the serializer's test rigs only, `serializer/tests/forin.lua:58-73` and three copies; the mod has no sweep, so a save that lands on a yield inside an `ipairs` loop body is refused, as it is on stock OC: the perms-flattener row, open). **`pairs` is closed** by the replay iterator (M3.1): the loop's position is never persisted, its remaining keys are, and the loop is despecialised on restore so a replacement iterator can drive it. Measured at 17 shapes × 64 hash-layout rotations = 1088 fresh-process restores, all exact (8 shapes through a third process, so the last restore resumes a loop already in replay form), against a negative control that diverges 59/64. A five-dimension adversarial review found 12 defects, 10 confirmed — including a critical one that only appears on the SECOND save through a perms prototype, which the single-round-trip suite structurally could not see. Stock OpenComputers still has this defect and fails silently (0/20).
- [x] **M4 — protocol + integration**: spkey with pre-reserved ref slots, `settings("spkey"/"path")`, perms/uperms; bridge-side ERIS-library analog; OpenOS boot → persist mid-`pullSignal` → restore → continue. Contract already verified against a real host's call sequence by [../serializer/tests/contract.lua](../serializer/tests/contract.lua) — note `configure()` runs at the top of **every** persist *and* unpersist, so `settings` must be re-settable. **Measured on a live machine and not obtainable by reading:** `machine.lua` overwrites `string.find/match/gmatch/gsub` with its own Lua closures *after* `PersistenceAPI` builds perms, so those four are serialized as **code** in essentially every real blob rather than resolved as permanents — and `gmatch` splits on `#s < SHORT_STRING` (500), tail-returning the unpersistable native C iterator below it and a persistable pure-Lua closure above. The gmatch refusal is therefore **data-length-dependent, not categorical**. **Done -- verified 2026-10-03:** jnlua's ERIS case opens `eris` through `luaopen_eris` (`native/lj52shim.c:2739`). `settings` takes `spkey`/`path` and can be set again (`serializer/eris_lj.c:2926-3016`, `tests/contract.lua:67-73`). Perms/uperms are OC's own `PersistenceAPI`, driven from `LuaJITArchitecture.java:475-523`/`:565-642`. OpenOS boots, persists and resumes the same VM in the harness (`c-openos-shell`, `f1-persist-blob`, `f2-restore-same-vm`, `f3-restore-counter-continues`, `f4-restore-no-error`) and in game (T1-T3). The spkey protocol shipped as shell-fill ([shell-fill.md](shell-fill.md), 2026-09-17): the special's final table is registered at its record, and recipes run after the whole graph exists (`eris_lj.c:3190`). The short-string `gmatch` refusal is still live: the kernel's `str_gmatch` returns the native iterator below 500 bytes (`build/native/kernel/machine.lua:599-600`), and `eris_lj.c:1697` refuses it. That fix belongs to the builtin-upvalue sweep row (moved there from the sandbox-constraints row 2026-10-10).
- [x] **M4.4 — function environments diverge from stock Eris. DECIDED 2026-09-19: keep persisting the fenv, always; no serializer change.** Every LuaJIT function carries an fenv and `getfenv(f) == _G` holds even for a closure referencing no global; 5.2 gives such a closure no `_ENV` upvalue at all. Measured on the differential rig: `eris.persist(f)` with an EMPTY perms table **succeeds on stock Eris and is refused by us** ("cannot persist a C function by value"); with `perms={[_G]='_G'}` both succeed and round-trip. **Why keep it.** (1) The divergence is narrower than it reads: in 5.2 the `_ENV` upvalue exists for any closure whose lexical subtree references a global, and it holds `_G`, which holds C functions, which Eris persists only through perms — so stock refuses too the moment a closure touches any global. The classes differ only for a closure whose entire lexical subtree is global-free, and only under a perms table lacking `_G`, which OC never supplies (`PersistenceAPI` flattens `_G` first). (2) In LuaJIT the fenv is real, observable state, not a compiler artefact: `getfenv`/`setfenv` read and write it, and `lj_func_newL_gc` gives every closure created by `f` the fenv `f` had at that moment — so a global-free `f` restored with a substituted environment would hand that environment to every closure it creates afterwards. OC loads all user code with the sandbox table as fenv; the only substitution that could shrink the blob ("fenv equals the writer's `_G`, restore the reader's") is exactly the implicit rebinding perms exists to make explicit and by name, and its failure mode on a mismatched reader is a closure silently reading a different global table. (3) What it costs in OC is one reference per closure record to a table that is already a permanent. Documented in `serializer/README.md` (design notes). The remaining asymmetry — that perms is load-bearing for us where stock could omit it — is the contract, and hosts that flatten `_G` (all of them) never see it.
- [x] **M4.5, THE KERNEL HALF — the two probability-one silent corruptions are gone, 2026-09-19.** The census's #1 (`component.list`, `machine.lua:1309-1319`) and #3 (`componentProxy.__pairs`, `machine.lua:1361-1381`) each kept a `next` cursor in a closure upvalue, invisible to the replay scan: a save landing inside the loop resumed against the restored table's different hash layout and silently visited the wrong keys. Measured fail-first in the harness on the 9-site kernel: `fi-2` restored walk 6 of 7 components with one duplicated, `fi-3` restored walk stopped AT the saved key, 16 of 33 proxy methods — the "device table quietly disagrees with reality" mode — and the same run on **stock OC's own PUC+Eris arm gave list 9 keys with 2 duplicates, pairs 42 keys with 2 missing and 11 duplicated**, so the built-in architecture's looser version of this defect is now measured, not assumed. Kernel sites 10–11 snapshot each walk into an array walked by integer index — the one shape whose position is a plain integer the replay never needs to see. Site 10 (`component.list`) re-reads `list[key]` at call time so a key cleared mid-walk is skipped exactly as `next` would; site 11 (`componentProxy.__pairs`) snapshots both phases (own keys minus `fields`, then `fields`) into one `{k, v}` array through the raw `next, self` triple, because `pairs(self)` would recurse into the metamethod. OC's three `next(` calls all lived inside these two sites, so `build-kernel.sh` now asserts `snapshot walks=2` and `next( calls=0`: a survivor means OC grew an iterator we do not know about. API preserved by construction and by test: the return is still a callable table with a `{__call}` metatable, `list("gpu")()` still yields the first `(address, type)` pair, exhaustion returns nothing and the next call restarts, and on identical tables both new walkers visit the identical key SEQUENCE OC's did (a stub rig ran the patched and original blocks side by side on one LuaJIT). Gate: kernel rebuild byte-stable (51147 B, 11 sites), harness 45/0 with the new `fi-1/2/3` milestones (restored walks element-wise equal to never-saved walks, 7/7 and 33/33), the NEGATIVE CONTROL with sites 10–11 cut out fails `fi-2` and `fi-3` with the wrong-sequence symptom and nothing else (43 unchanged), `run-forin.sh` 22 cases (`oclist_snap`/`ocpairs_snap` 20/20 exact; `oclist`/`ocpairs` diverging as the controls must), `wd_test` 32/0. Serializer untouched: the serializer hash did not move and the shipped DLL is the same `49ead6cc`. Reviewer swept all 14 `component.list` callers in OpenOS plus the kernel's own and the one `pairs(proxy)` caller (`bin/components.lua`): none mutates the list mid-walk, none depends on order or on live component changes. Two accepted divergences, both documented at the sites: a key added to the list table between `list()` and the walk is no longer visited, and site 11 returns the value captured at `pairs()` time rather than re-reading it (proxy values are method closures that do not change mid-walk). **In-game confirmed 2026-09-19** (T3 in [in-game-tests.md](in-game-tests.md)): both walks saved mid-loop and reloaded, 7/7 components and 33/33 gpu-proxy keys each exactly once, log clean.
- [x] **M4.5, THE RESIDUAL — #9, the OS-authored `next`-wrapper — THE DIAGNOSTIC SHIPPED 2026-09-22.** `eris.settings("forin", "ignore"|"warn"|"refuse")` (default ignore, blob bytes unchanged — asserted against a fixture written by the previous serializer, compared after the fingerprint header) and `eris.diagnostics()` (returns and clears the pending list); the scan flags, once per loop, a Lua-closure iterator inside or at its loop whose prototype does a `GGET` of `next`, or holds the `next` fast function in an upvalue, or holds a Lua closure that does (one level), and names BOTH locations — the loop's `chunk:line` and the iterator's `chunk:linedefined`; the snapshot-array walkers of kernel sites 10–11 are not flagged, a closure calling `next` on some other table is the documented false positive. The mod sets the mode from `-Docluajit.forin=<mode>` after `super.initialize()` and drains the list after every successful save to the server log, once per distinct message per machine. Harness `dg-1`: with `-Docluajit.forin=warn`, a `for k in pairs(component)` loop parked mid-walk (OpenOS's own `boot/04_component.lua` `__pairs`) is named on save — `for-in loop at /mnt/.../autorun.lua:237 iterates with a Lua closure (boot/04_component.lua:18) that calls next; its position is not replayable and resumes against a different hash layout after a reload` — and with the property absent nothing is looked at (`0 diagnostics, as configured`). **In-game confirmed 2026-09-22** (T6 in [in-game-tests.md](in-game-tests.md): one WARN line naming `stdin:1` and `boot/04_component.lua:18`). The original residual text follows. OpenOS's own `boot/04_component.lua` installs a `__pairs` on the `component` LIBRARY table of exactly this shape (a closure over `next` with a `parent` phase flag, walking the table and then `primaries`), and any OS may write one; at save time it is indistinguishable from a legitimate custom iterator, so it cannot be rewritten, only detected. `for k in pairs(component)` is rare, and neither sites 10–11 nor `fi-3` cover it. Planned: an opt-in save-time diagnostic that warns when a for-in iterator is a Lua closure whose body reaches `next` on its own loop state, plus the written contract. Bounded by [research/pg-stock-oc-parity.md](research/pg-stock-oc-parity.md): stock OC loses EVERY suspended `pairs` loop (0/20 exact), so this residual is a strict subset of what the built-in architectures already lose. Design and tiers in [forin-iterator-gap.md](forin-iterator-gap.md); T2/T3 not built. **2026-10-03:** the "written contract" planned above was never written; it is tracked with the player-facing docs row. T2 is decided against in effect: [forin-iterator-gap.md](forin-iterator-gap.md):10-12 makes the diagnostic the answer, "not another rewrite", though that document's own open question (:369-373) was never closed.
- [ ] **M5 — hardening**: persist→unpersist→persist oracle in CI, randomized state fuzzing, corrupted-blob rejection, canary self-test on every load, GC-stress soak with LuaJIT assertions **Status 2026-10-03:** corrupted-blob rejection is done. Magic, format, build fingerprint and CRC32 are checked before the body is parsed (`serializer/eris_lj.c:3165-3183`), and the gates are `tests/m1.lua:325-361` (every prefix of a valid blob refused), the CRC-valid crafted blobs from `m1.lua:399` and `tests/m2.lua:296`. The persist→unpersist→persist oracle exists (`m1.lua:377-390`, `m2.lua:330-340`, `m3.lua:657`) but runs only under `make test`: CI is GTNH's gradle workflow on a runner with no C toolchain (`build.gradle.kts:28-31`). Still open: randomized fuzzing, the canary structure persisted beside the kernel ([persistence-study.md](persistence-study.md):55), and a GC-stress soak on an assertion-enabled LuaJIT. The tree has none of these three. **Also open, from the 2026-10-03 review:** (a) a GC-pressure round trip does exist, `serializer/shell-fill/shell_stress.lua` case B (`:45-81`: 300 specials in a deep coroutine graph, the collector forced aggressive, 20 round trips), but nothing runs it (`make test` does not, `serializer/Makefile:43-49`), so what is missing is that, and the long soak on a `LUA_USE_ASSERT`/`LUA_USE_APICHECK` build, which nothing in the tree configures; (b) prototypes are not deduplicated (`serializer/README.md:113-118`): N closures of one prototype come back as N prototypes, so a restored machine can be charged more RAM than it held at the save; (c) the ocelot-brain harness is itself outside CI ("a manual harness outside CI", the unpersist-order row), and whether it belongs in CI is undecided. **Status 2026-10-10:** Takes over the leftovers of the shell-fill redesign row (`PERSISTENCE, REDESIGNED FROM REQUIREMENTS`, ticked 2026-10-10). (1) `make test` (`serializer/Makefile:43-49`) runs none of `tests/shell.lua`, `tests/shell-order.lua` and `shell-fill/shell_stress.lua`; each prints `FAILS: 0` run by hand on `serializer/erislj_test.exe` (serializer hash `8c5a1168`, the source at HEAD; run 2026-10-10, with M1 82, M2 56, M3 79, `contract` and `stack-universe` passing beside them), so one Makefile edit closes this and the first half of (a). (2) The D4 rig port: `serializer/shell-fill/rig.lua` (2026-09-17) has only `RIG_MODE=orig` and `snap` (`:12-24`), so `serializer/tests/userdata.lua` still needs the `RIG_MODE=shell` mirror that `docs/shell-fill.md:362-366` specifies. (3) The `udinvoke` nil-guard (`docs/shell-fill.md:366`) is in neither `native/kernel/patch-machine-lua.lua` nor the kernel (`build/native/kernel/machine.lua:1151-1157`); by reading, a load cannot produce a Value-less proxy, because OC's `userdata.load` throws rather than returning nil (`luac/UserdataAPI.scala:36-54` in the 1.12.55-GTNH sources) and a recipe that throws fails the whole load (`serializer/eris_lj.c:2276-2281`), so the choice is to build it or drop it. (4) `serializer/tests/stack-universe.lua` cites the kernel as of 2026-09-19, now 21 to 85 lines early: header `:7`, `:24` (`wrapUserdataInto` `:1213` is `:1237`) and the mirror's comments `:111-265`; site 13 (85faa0e) put `ocljJitWasOn` (kernel `:20`) ahead of every chunk local, which moved each slot by one and kept their order, so the mirror holds. Also: `make test` links `prototype/watchdog/libluajit_stock.a` (`serializer/Makefile:4-6`), built without CHECKHOOK (`prototype/watchdog/Makefile:17`, `:54-57`) and without the penalty scrub and errmem clamp that `native/build-native.sh:454`, `:488` apply to the shipped copy, so neither the oracle nor a soak on that library tests the LuaJIT we ship; and a CI job must fetch LuaJIT 1ee778a4 itself, since `prototype/watchdog/luajit/` is gitignored (`.gitignore:43`). **Status 2026-10-16:** the fingerprint check now names the field that differs and has its own test (`tests/fingerprint.lua`, in `make test`); `make test` links the build copy's `libluajit.a` (the shipped feature set, CHECKHOOK and the LuaJIT patches) instead of `prototype/watchdog/libluajit_stock.a`; the `eris_lj.c` line cites above moved with the word's insertion (`:3165-3183` is now `:3291-3309`, `:2276-2281` is `:2350-2355`). **Status 2026-10-20:** one more item for this row, from the glibc round's for-in flake (the glibc row's item 5): a thread blob is not deterministic across processes. Its slot loop writes raw words that the restore never reads, each Lua frame's link (its return PC) and, for a continuation frame, its `lj_cont_*` code address and its `contpc`; `u_thread`'s pass 3 overwrites them all (`eris_lj.c:2762-2769` for a continuation frame) [S][M: decoded Alma blobs, scratch `claude-tmp/oclj/glibc/followup/inv-forin/alma-2000/dump-37.B.txt`]. The code address also tells where the library was loaded [I]. `serializer/README.md`'s known limitation leaves writing nil there as its own item, a wire change; not started. Until then `diag/4-identity` masks the link, and a byte-for-byte oracle across processes cannot exist.

## Parked — not on the active list

*Rows here are not being worked on. Each says what would bring it back. Moved here 2026-10-10.*

- [ ] **`timerfd` + one process-wide epoll poller — the named escalation, with a named trigger.** Take it on a report of thread pressure or roughly 200+ concurrent machines, not before. It is a drop-in behind the five-function platform seam, which is why the seam is shaped that way. Today's cost is ~25-30 KB and one thread per machine, against a JVM already running 100+ threads and OC's own per-machine executors — and `docs/watchdog.md:39` already requires a dedicated per-machine runner thread for stage-4 abandonment, so this is not a new class of cost. **Parked 2026-10-10:** waits on its own trigger, which cannot fire before a release (no tag exists at a4994e6). Two refinements, re-checked at HEAD: "one thread per machine" holds on Linux only (one eager `pthread_create` per state, `lj52shim.c:1813`, on a 128 KB stack, `:1478`), while the Win32 backend arms `CreateTimerQueueTimer` on the default queue (`:1755`), which the system's shared pool serves; and the stage-4 runner thread `docs/watchdog.md:39` requires is a design, not built (the hard-abort row), which if built would double Linux's threads per machine. On macOS the analogue would be a kqueue `EVFILT_TIMER` poller.
- [ ] **A macOS backend.** Needs `pthread_cond_timedwait_relative_np` or a Mach timer in place of `pthread_condattr_setclock`. Blocked on having a machine to build and test on; the `#error` refuses until then. **Parked 2026-10-10: macOS is unsupported for now** (the user's decision: no Mac to build and test on). On a Mac the jar finds no native for the platform, logs once and registers no LuaJIT CPU (`OCLuaJIT.java:59-68`); OpenComputers' own CPUs keep working, since GTNH's `OpenComputers-1.12.64-GTNH.jar` ships darwin-x86_64 and darwin-aarch64 natives (verified 2026-10-10). The row names only the watchdog backend (the `#error` is still at `lj52shim.c:60`). A Mac build also needs the one-argument `pthread_setname_np` (`:1819` calls glibc's two-argument form), `MACOSX_DEPLOYMENT_TARGET` (LuaJIT's `src/Makefile:320-322` stops without it, and `build-native.sh` never sets it), a Mach-O reader in the postflight (`build-native.sh:813-814`), and a decision on `MAP_JIT` under a hardened JVM (`lj_mcode.c:117`). The gates run the freshly built `luajit` (`build-native.sh:532-555`), so it needs a Mac host or hosted macOS runners. The native naming already matches OC's (`build-native.sh:177-193`, `:218`).
- [ ] Platform matrix completion: linux-aarch64, mac-x64/arm64 **Status 2026-10-03:** `build/native/dist` holds win-x64 and linux-x64 only. mac-x64/arm64 is blocked on the macOS row (the `#error` at `lj52shim.c:60`). linux-aarch64 is more than a build: the watchdog's cross-thread injection relies on x86-TSO for its ordering (`lj52shim.c:1583`, "Order matters and x86-TSO keeps it"), so it needs explicit barriers or a review for a weakly ordered CPU, and `wd_test` on real aarch64 hardware. Every platform also needs a native build host, because the build's gates run the `luajit` it has just built. **Parked 2026-10-10: linux-aarch64 and macOS are unsupported for now** (the user's decision: no ARM or Mac hardware). The supported platforms are windows-x86_64 and linux-x86_64. Elsewhere the LuaJIT CPU is not offered (`OCLuaJIT.java:59-68`), while GTNH's OpenComputers ships its own CPUs' natives for linux-aarch64, linux-arm and darwin-aarch64 (verified 2026-10-10), so ARM servers and Apple Silicon players keep OC's CPUs. What a port would take, re-checked: the LuaJIT side is portable C (CHECKHOOK is upstream's own `#ifdef` in `lj_record.c`; the two patches touch `lj_func.c` and `lj_err.c`, and the stale-top clamp covers every VM's fast functions by construction, though only `vm_x64.dasc` was read for the pattern, `bench/results-errmem-2026-10-05.md`), and the Linux watchdog backend builds for aarch64 as it is. The real work is the weak-ordering review of `lj52_wd_inject` (`lj52shim.c:1585-1591`: plain stores of `hookf` and `hookcount`, a sequentially consistent `__atomic_fetch_or` on `hookmask`, no acquire on the VM thread), which darwin-arm64 needs as much as linux-aarch64 and which needs no hardware; then `wd_test` and the harness on real ARM hardware or hosted arm64 runners. **Two JIT facts for a port, found 2026-10-21** [I: from the instruction sets' documented behaviour; S: arm64's `asm_conv` emits `FCVTZS` for an int64 conversion of a double, `lj_asm_arm64.h` ~:658-659 at the pin, as its `vm_num2i64` does, `vm_arm64.dasc` ~:2197-2198; FIX-NUMCONV step 1's `argument.md` section 6, scratch `claude-tmp/oclj/numconv/argue/`]: FIX-NUMCONV's argument holds on x64 only, because arm64's `FCVTZS` saturates, so +inf or a double of 2^63 or more converts to 2^63 - 1, which is no double, and a port must argue that rule again (JIT guide 3.11); and round 3's operand guard would accept x = 2^63 there (`FCVTZS` gives 2^63 - 1, `SCVTF` of that rounds back to 2^63 = x, so the `EQ.num` passes) where 5.3.6 raises `number has no integer representation`.

## Open questions

*None is open as of 2026-10-10: each bullet below is answered, decided, or moved into a row.*

- OC version pin: currently `1.12.58-GTNH` (2026-08-30); revisit at v1 start. **2026-10-03:** still the compile pin (`dependencies.gradle:39`); the jar has run in game on 1.12.61-GTNH and 1.12.64-GTNH. Bounding the range at run time is the `@Mod` row. **Status 2026-10-10:** The same item as the `@Mod` row, which carries both the compile pin (still `dependencies.gradle:39`, 1.12.58-GTNH) and the versions run in game; nothing is open here beyond it.
- Whether to shim JNLua's Java API (reuse OC's `LuaState` abstractions) or keep a minimal purpose-built JNI surface like CCLuaJIT. Current lean: minimal purpose-built. **Decided 2026-09-01, the other way: reuse.** OC's own `jnlua.c`, repacked, runs over our `lj52shim` behind a fourth `LuaState` class, `LuaStateLuaJIT` ([research/jnlua-binding.md](research/jnlua-binding.md); the v0.5 split row).
- `bit32` shim semantics: LuaJIT `bit.*` returns signed 32-bit; bit32 is unsigned — normalize with `% 2^32`; needs a conformance test against PUC `bit32`. **Answered:** `bit32` is a real 5.2 implementation in C (`lj52shim.c:2579-2733`), not a normalised alias of `bit`, and it is differentially tested, 45/47 exact against PUC 5.2 (the `bit32` row and the kernel row near the end of v1).
- CHECKHOOK's measured overhead on hot loops (prototype task) — if it's large, consider offering both native builds (safe vs fast) as a server config. **Answered:** negligible on real work ([watchdog.md](watchdog.md):77: mandelbrot 58.4 ms against 59.0 ms on the stock build; a degenerate numeric/bit microloop measured 29.0 against 24.0 ms, ~20% in one noisy run, on the same line), and a build without CHECKHOOK cannot boot OC's kernel at all (the native-build-pipeline row), so there is no fast build to offer.
- Microcontrollers/robots/drones: verify `@Architecture.NoMemoryRequirements` interaction and tier gating for the new CPU option. **Still open -- 2026-10-03:** no microcontroller, robot or drone has run our architecture (none appears in [in-game-tests.md](in-game-tests.md) T1-T9), and `LuaJITArchitecture` carries only `@Architecture.Name` (`LuaJITArchitecture.java:82`). The kernel's own footprint does not come out of a device's RAM: OC grants it on top of installed RAM (`NativeLuaArchitecture.scala:158`, `:221` in the 1.12.55-GTNH sources). At OC's 1.8 a 192 KB machine holds at least what stock holds (the RAM-scale row; LuaJIT GC64 objects are mostly smaller, not larger), but what these devices meet first is the collector's 128 KiB watermark floor on a tiny cap (the collector-at-the-wall row). None of them has been measured. **Moved 2026-10-10 into the in-game validation row.**
